It was left unbuilt deliberately, because sending a client's name, number and property parish to a third party is a new destination for personal data and CLAUDE.md section 10 puts that with her. The counter-argument was put once, that it is a second source of truth for a job when the desk already works, and she asked for it anyway. That is the decision, and it is recorded here as hers.

It is an HTTP hop rather than a function call: `yaad-inbound` is Deno on Supabase, the HubSpot client is TypeScript on Cloudflare Workers, and a Deno copy would be a second implementation of CRM writes free to drift from the first, which is the reasoning `/api/hubspot/stage` already records. So the function signs a small payload and posts it to a new `/api/hubspot/lead`, same signing scheme, its own secret, and there stays one place that knows how to talk to HubSpot.

**The field list is the data protection boundary and it is short on purpose.** Name, the number they messaged from, parish, trade, urgency, the job code, the channel. The description does NOT leave, and it is the important omission: it is whatever somebody typed on WhatsApp, and it routinely carries the address, who is alone in the house, when the house is empty, and a voice note transcribed in their own words. None of that is needed to run a sales pipeline. The address, the photographs and the client email are held back for the same reason. `web/tests/hubspot-lead.test.mjs` asserts that a payload carrying those fields still cannot get them into HubSpot, so widening the list fails the build rather than passing quietly. Widening it is her decision, not a future session's.

**It fires once, on a job the client has confirmed**, never on a greeting and never mid-conversation, and it runs after the reply has already gone. Every failure is a log line: a CRM that missed a lead is a worse day, a client who was not answered is a worse business. `yaadly_job_id` on the deal is the idempotency key, searched before every write, because a redelivery or a second confirmation would otherwise create a second deal and quietly overstate the pipeline, which is the one number a January funding conversation turns on. The whole feature is off until `HUBSPOT_LEAD_WEBHOOK_SECRET` is set in both places, so nothing reaches HubSpot until she switches it on.

**Applied and proved, later the same session, on her word.** `20260904f` went in and the rig ran: 17 of 17 pass against the live database, all four functions now carry `same_phone()`, none still carries the last-nine comparison, and the fixtures cleaned up after themselves. The rig earned its keep before it proved anything it was written for: the kickoff pack fixture was refused twice by guards nobody had told it about, once for payment stages not totalling 100 percent and once by `kickoff_approval_attributed`, which is the governing rule of this project (a named human confirms every consequential step) enforced in Postgres rather than in a prompt. Both refusals were the database being right and the test being wrong, and the fixture was corrected rather than the guards.

**One correction to what the lead sync could assume.** The live portal was checked before the code was finished, and it was worth checking: most of the properties `hubspotConfig.ts` describes do not exist in it. That file is a specification of what should be created, not a record of what has been, and reading it as the latter would have failed the first real lead with a 400 naming a property nobody had made. HubSpot rejects a whole write for one unrecognised property, so the sync was rebuilt to use standard properties plus exactly one custom key, `yaadly_job_id`, with parish, trade, urgency and channel written into the standard `description` as a sentence. One setup step instead of six, and a test that fails the build if a second custom property is ever added without being created. Its own test also caught a real ordering flaw: the contact was written before the idempotency key was checked, so a portal missing that property would have left a trail of contacts behind on every retry. Nothing is written now until the thing that prevents duplicates is known to work.

**Everything the database and HubSpot needed is now in place, and only the deploy is left.** All six migrations from this work are applied and verified: `same_phone()` and the four WhatsApp RPCs, the inbound ledger index and sweep, the three new `jobs` columns with their backfill, `intake_threads.desk_notes` with the `my_intake_threads` view, and the `intake_threads` create-table migration that is a deliberate no-op against the live table. Afterwards: 40 jobs and 5 threads intact, both thread policies intact, and the backfill reading 12 whatsapp, 11 form, 11 test, 3 web, 1 email, with 2 legacy rows left blank rather than guessed into a channel.

On the HubSpot side the deal property `yaadly_job_id` was created by the founder and verified queryable, and the `presentationscheduled` stage was renamed from "Funds Held" to "Client Paid" through the API, label only, with `displayOrder` and `metadata` read first and sent back unchanged so the PATCH could not reorder the board or alter the stage's win probability. That rename was found only because the property was being verified: `hubspotConfig.ts` had been corrected in code hours earlier and that file has never had control over the labels, so finding 13 had been called done when only half of it was. Worth remembering, because it is the general shape of the mistake: a specification file is not a record of the live system, and the audit made that error twice in one day, once about HubSpot properties and once about stage labels.

**The secret is set in both places and the feature is still off**, because the code is on a branch. Setting it was deliberate preparation rather than a switch being thrown: `yaad-inbound` and the app both read it, neither is deployed with this code, and creating `yaadly_job_id` in HubSpot is the one step left before a deploy would do anything.

**Superseded note, kept because the reasoning is the useful part.** Until she approved it, the three RPCs read as follows.

**The three remaining WhatsApp RPCs are written, tested on paper, and NOT applied.** `20260904f` converts `agree_quote_via_whatsapp`, `agree_kickoff_pack_via_whatsapp` and `choose_worker_via_whatsapp` to `same_phone()`, each body read out of production with `pg_get_functiondef` first rather than trusted from a migration file. `supabase/tests/whatsapp_phone_guards.sql` is the rig for them, and it is built so that no positive case is ever allowed to succeed: the fixture is a job with no quotes and no pack, so a correct number gets past the phone check and fails at the next one with a different message. Two different refusals is the proof, and nothing moves money.

Applying the migration and running the rig were both refused by the environment's own safeguards, which is the right call on three security-definer functions that book workers and agree prices. What did get applied is `20260904b`: `same_phone()` itself, and `approve_stage_via_whatsapp`, the one that raises a worker payable. Verified live and read-only afterwards: the old rule really did collide on the test pair, `same_phone` refuses that collision, still accepts a dropped country code, and `approve_stage_via_whatsapp` is converted while the other three are not. So the sharpest edge is closed and the remaining three are one approved migration away, behind the tightened candidate gate in `yaad-inbound` in the meantime.

---

## 2026-09-04 · The WhatsApp agent audit, carried out

A full review of the WhatsApp intake agent and its end to end workflow was run on 4 September 2026 and produced twenty findings. The founder's instruction was to carry out all of them. This entry records the choices inside that work that were not simply "do what the audit said", because those are the ones that will look arbitrary later.

**The agent was describing a business that stopped existing on 3 September, and the screen could not catch it.** Its own fact list told clients "money is held and released stage by stage" and "Yaadly does not price the work". The second sentence is the exact one `docs/marketplace.html` retired the day the principal structure was settled, for the stated reason that a client receiving a quote from a worker is a client contracting with that worker. The first walked straight past `guardrails.scan`, because the banned pattern was `we hold your money`, active voice only, and the passive is the form a model actually writes. So the prompt was supplying the one phrase the screen exists to stop. Both facts were rewritten to the principal story, `NO_PRICE_SENTENCE` in `price-figures.ts` went with them (it fires exactly when a client has asked about money, which is the worst possible place for a retired claim), and the same defect was found and fixed a second time in `yaad-notify-client`'s reporting prompt.

**The new guardrail patterns are deliberately narrow, and one line was deliberately not touched.** They ban the claim that the client's money is sitting with Yaadly awaiting release, which is the escrow reading. They do not touch "held safely with a licensed payment provider", because CLAUDE.md section 8 prescribes that phrase by name, and CLAUDE.md is the founder's file. Worth her eye: after the principal move, that prescribed phrase sits oddly with a structure where the client buys the job outright, and settling it is hers rather than a session's.

**A client was being told the worker had been paid, and the worker had not been paid.** Approving a stage over WhatsApp replied "and the worker is paid for it". What approval actually does is fire `raise_worker_pay_invoice_on_stage_approval`, and both documents are still admin-raised and admin-marked-paid off platform (3 Sep entry below), with the published FAQ promising three working days. The message is now "the worker's payment is raised".

**Two lanes were greedy and trapping people, and the fix is a way out rather than a narrowing.** A client with one job mid review had every message filed as a comment on that job; a worker with one active job had every message filed as evidence and drafted into a client report. Both behaviours are right and both had no exit. `escape-hatch.ts` gives each one: a client naming a different job, property or problem falls through to a fresh intake, and either side asking for a person falls through to the handover path that already exists. Nothing bespoke was built for what happens after the hatch opens, on purpose, because the ordinary pipeline already saves the message, pushes to her phone and hands the thread over, and a third copy of that would drift from the first two. Most of the hatch's tests are negative: a false positive means a real complaint silently stops being filed, which fails in the direction nobody checks.

**`human_handling` now stops the auto-filing lanes but not the four job-code lanes.** The flag was honoured only in the client-intake pipeline at the bottom of the function, so every lane above it had already run: a client mid conversation with Monique could still have a message filed as a comment and pushed to a worker without her seeing it. The thread read moved up. The four money actions (agree a quote, agree a Kickoff Pack, book a worker, approve a stage) deliberately stay open while she holds a thread, because those are a person naming a job's own code, which is an instruction rather than a remark, and a client who asked to speak to her on Tuesday should still be able to approve a stage on Thursday.

**The inbound ledger and the throttle share one table, which is what its own migration said would happen.** `wa_inbound_seen` was created on 30 August for the Meta webhook, that webhook was deleted on 1 September, and the function README went on describing the table as the live retry defence for three days while nothing read or wrote it. It is now written from `yaad-inbound`, keyed on Twilio's `MessageSid` and Resend's `email_id`, before any state changes. The same rows answer "how many messages has this number sent in the last hour", which is the throttle the WhatsApp door did not have while the website chat door had three. One table, two jobs, one sweep.

**The desk's pause switch stops the model and nothing else.** The concierge had been telling the truth about this gap in its own words since it was built, and naming the fix. `yaad-inbound` now reads `app_settings.agents_paused` and, when it is on, skips the model call, keeps the message, keeps the photographs, pushes to her phone and tells the client a person is picking it up. Pausing the record as well would be a switch that loses work, which nobody would ever dare use.

**Phone identity: tightened in TypeScript everywhere, in SQL in one place, and the boundary is stated rather than hidden.** Every match in this path was the last nine digits, which makes two numbers in different countries the same person, and that check guards a worker pay invoice. `samePhone()` requires a full match or a difference no larger than a country code, and is strictly tighter than what it replaces. All twelve comparisons in `yaad-inbound` use it. On the database side, `same_phone()` was added and only `approve_stage_via_whatsapp` was redefined to use it, its live body read out of production first rather than trusted from a migration file. `agree_quote_via_whatsapp`, `agree_kickoff_pack_via_whatsapp` and `choose_worker_via_whatsapp` still carry the old form internally and are now behind the tightened candidate gate, so a mismatched number no longer reaches them. Rewriting five security-definer money functions in one sweep, without running `supabase/tests` against them, is its own reviewed piece of work and should not ride along in an audit.

**A failed job write used to cost the client everything they had typed.** It returned JSON to Twilio, which reads TwiML, so nothing was sent and from their side the message had vanished; and the transcript upsert sat below the failing line, so the conversation went too. The conversation is now saved first, they get a real reply on their own channel, and an urgent push goes to Monique, because this is the one failure nobody else would ever notice. The uncaught handler at the bottom of the file had the identical bug while its own comment described the bug, and now answers in TwiML too.

**`jobs` gained `source`, `intake_turns` and `handed_to_human`, and `intake_threads` finally got a migration.** Channel lived in the primary key prefix and in English inside the description, so "how many jobs came from WhatsApp" could not be asked of the database. The backfill separates `JOB-WEB-` into `form` and `web` using the presence of a web conversation, because two different functions mint that prefix. `intake_threads`, the record of every conversation Yaadly has ever had, had been created by hand in the dashboard: on a free plan with no point-in-time recovery its shape existed in exactly one place. The migration was written from the live table and is a no-op against it.

**HubSpot's configuration was corrected; HubSpot was not wired up.** `FUNDS_HELD` became `CLIENT_PAID` and the board label with it, because that file's own header warns that stage labels reach clients through workflow emails, and the service dropdown lost three prices that were all wrong by the time anybody looked. The wire event `payment_held` is kept as an alias for `client_paid`, since a sender configured outside this repository would otherwise start getting 400s silently. What was deliberately NOT done is making a WhatsApp lead create a contact or a deal: that is a new destination for client personal data, which CLAUDE.md section 10 puts with the founder and not with a session, and the audit's own recommendation was that it is a second source of truth for a job when one desk already works. Flagged, not built.

**The model call was split in two, and the timeout objection turned out to be the design.** This was first left undone on the reasoning that a second sequential call makes an existing latency problem worse. That reasoning had the diagnosis backwards. The single call was slow *because* it did both jobs: extraction, which wants temperature 0 and a rigid shape, and writing, which wants warmth and no shape at all. Its token budget went 1100, then 3500, then 6000, each rise recorded in this file's own comments, each time because the model spent the whole budget reasoning through both jobs inside a `<think>` block and never reached the JSON. Every one of those cost a real client a real answer.

So `classifyTheJob()` runs at temperature 0 with a 2000 token budget and a fixed set of short fields, and `composeReply()` writes plain text with no JSON envelope to reach at all, which means the "answered in prose and we threw it away" failure cannot happen to it. The prose fallback that patched that failure is gone, and its absence is the point.

**The real fix underneath it is that somebody now owns the total.** Chasing the timeout question properly turned up a worse problem than the one being asked about. Every slow step in this function carried its own fixed timeout, set in isolation and sensible on its own: transcription 90 seconds, the model 25, a media fetch 45, three notification pushes at 4 apiece with several awaited on the critical path. Twilio abandons an inbound webhook at about fifteen seconds and the sender then gets nothing. Any one of the first three is past that on its own, which means a voice note, which this file's own comment calls "how most of these actually arrive", could not reliably be answered at all. Nobody had ever added them up.

`deadline.ts` is one budget for the request. Every slow step asks for a slice of what is left and says what it must leave behind; a step with no room is not started. The reserves are ordered so that the last thing squeezed is the only thing with a fallback: transcription first because without it there is no content, then the classifier because without it there is no job, then the writer, which degrades into `reply-from-card.ts`. That fallback is a real reply built from the card, proving they were heard and asking for the right missing thing, rather than the fixed generic opener that asks somebody who has just described their roof what needs doing. **The floor is strictly better than the old design's: a timeout used to mean no card and no reply.** The arithmetic is walked through on a fake clock in `deadline_test.ts`, because four steps sharing twelve seconds is arithmetic, and arithmetic done in your head in a comment is how a budget ends up over-subscribed with nobody noticing. That test caught a real over-subscription in the first set of numbers.

Email is deliberately exempt and raises its own budget to ninety seconds. The limit exists because of Twilio and nothing else; a forwarded email has nobody holding a line open, and degrading that channel to meet a deadline that is not there would be a worse job for no reason.

**The three remaining WhatsApp RPCs are described above, and are the one thing still open.** Named rather than quietly dropped.

## 2026-09-03 · A migration in the repository is not a migration in the database

**The post-optimisation regression audit found a schema change that had been written, reviewed, committed and never applied.** `20260903g_a_face_to_the_name_and_the_intro_video_that_never_saved.sql` widened the `doc_type` constraint on `vetting_documents` to allow `profile_photo` and `intro_video`. Everything else in that chain was live: the browser recorded both, `yaad-vetting-upload` accepted both and put the file in the bucket, and `yaad-vetting-review` withheld both from the model. Only the constraint was missing, so every upload succeeded into storage and then failed at the row insert with "Could not record that document", leaving an orphaned file until the ninety day purge. It looks exactly like a bad connection, which is why nobody reported it. The irony is the point: that migration exists because somebody found this same bug for `intro_video` by reading the live constraint instead of the migration history, and then the fix was not applied either.

**So the standing check is: read the live schema, not the folder.** The folder is the intent; `supabase_migrations.schema_migrations` is the fact. The audit compared the two and found drift in both directions. Four repository migrations were unapplied, of which two were already live under different names and one, `20260901i`, is a real gap left open (see below). Two migrations were live and had never been committed at all, both touching the public `/ask` route, so the folder could not have rebuilt the database. Those two are written back as `20260903d2` and `20260903d3`, reconstructed verbatim from the live statements rather than from memory, including the revert, because the sequence is the lesson: phase two was applied while the deployed app still inserted directly, `/ask` broke for real visitors, and it was put back within the hour. `20260903j` does it a third time and records the checks that make it safe, which are that `ask_question()` is SECURITY DEFINER owned by `postgres`, that `postgres` owns `questions`, and that the table has no FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY, so the function does not consult a policy at all and cannot be broken by removing one.

**`worker_guidelines_version` is the one still open, and it is deliberately not being fixed quietly.** `web/lib/legal-copy.json` ships Worker Guidelines v1.4, which removed a police record check and a trial job that Yaadly does not actually do. The database still says 1.3. `match_workers_for_job` requires a worker's signed `doc_version` to equal `current_doc_version('worker_guidelines')` exactly, so today a worker signing the v1.4 text on screen records 1.4 and matches nothing, while the two workers who signed the removed v1.3 text keep matching. Both halves of that are wrong. Applying `20260901i` fixes the first and breaks the second: those two existing signatures stop matching until the workers re-sign. That is a decision about what counts as a valid signature, which is CLAUDE.md §10's territory and the founder's call, not an agent's.

**RLS gates rows, not columns, and that is now the third time it has bitten.** `20260903f` closed it on `worker_profiles`, `worker_checks` and `portfolio`; the audit found `public.answers` had the same shape and had been missed, so `worker_email` was readable from the open internet for any answer to a published question. Closed in `20260903i` with column grants rather than a view, because nothing joins to `answers` and `/ask` renders answers anonymously, so the view machinery `20260903f` needed would be ceremony here. The page also stopped selecting the column: a public page reading with the publishable key should not name a private column even when it never renders it. Nothing had leaked, because there were zero published questions, which is luck and not design.

**A `loading.tsx` turns a 404 into a 200, and that is not obvious from reading either file.** `/workers/[slug]` calls `notFound()` correctly, but the loading skeleton added in the same run puts a Suspense boundary in front of the page, so Next flushes the shell with a 200 before the database has said whether the worker exists. The reader still gets the right page; a search engine is told a profile exists for every slug ever typed. The fix is to resolve the profile in `generateMetadata`, which runs before the shell is flushed, and to leave the page's own `notFound()` as the backstop. Anyone adding a `loading.tsx` to a route that can 404 inherits this, so check the status code and not just the rendered page.

## 2026-09-03 · Concierge and preview realigned to the brand; a spacing and radius scale added, additive only

**Concierge and preview were the last two surfaces still on the pre-rebrand teal, mango and green identity.** `docs/yaadly.css` and `web/app/globals.css` already carried the purple/gold palette and Fraunces/IBM Plex (see the 2 and 3 Sep entries below); `concierge/concierge.html` and `preview/index.html` did not, each with its own `:root` still pointing `--teal`/`--mango`/`--coral` at the old hues and loading Anton, Space Grotesk and JetBrains Mono. Both moved onto the same values, values only, not names: `tone_()`/`chip_()` in concierge and every `.chip.ok/.warn/.crit` rule in both files reference `--teal`/`--mango`/`--coral` by name, so nothing downstream needed to change. Concierge keeps its own dense, at-a-desk layout; this was a palette and type swap, not a redesign. About 80 hardcoded hex and rgba literals outside each file's `:root` (gradients, chip fills, thumbnail placeholders) were swept to match, one deliberately left alone: concierge's `.chip.ai` already used its own purple, `#B39DFF`, unrelated to the teal/mango/coral system, and now sits next to a "proven" purple in the same family. That is the founder's call, not a default to silently pick either way, so the hex was left untouched pending it. Two more exclusions, both intentional and unrelated to the palette: concierge's `.msg.err` red (`#E5484D`) is its own error colour, never mango/teal/coral, and `.jichip.ready`'s dark amber fill is a generic tint that reads correctly against either the old or new gold, so neither was touched.

**`preview/index.html` had one place a CSS sweep could not reach: a JavaScript array of literal hex gradients.** `var GRAD=[...]` held four hardcoded gradient strings assigned to worker and quote avatar badges at runtime, which is exactly how this array went stale the first time the palette changed. It now builds itself from `getComputedStyle(document.documentElement)` reading `--teal`/`--tealb`/`--mango`/`--coral`/`--sand`, so the next palette change can't miss it a second time. The WhatsApp mock UI in the same file (and the WhatsApp launcher in `docs/chat.js`) keeps WhatsApp's real brand green, `#25D366`, on purpose in both places; neither is part of the Yaadly palette and neither should ever be swapped by a future palette pass that greps for old brand hex without reading what it's touching.

**A spacing, border-radius and breakpoint scale was added to `web/app/globals.css` and `docs/yaadly.css`, additive only.** Neither a formal scale existed anywhere before this: radius alone was 7 to 14px depending on the rule, and 13 different breakpoints were in live use across the two files. The new `--space-*`, `--radius-*` and `--bp-*` custom properties are deliberately plain `:root` properties, not inside Tailwind v4's `@theme` block in `globals.css`: `@theme` is what Tailwind auto-generates utility classes from, and defining `--radius-sm`/`--radius-md` etc. there would have silently redefined Tailwind's own `rounded-sm`/`rounded-xl` scale and reshaped every existing rounded corner in the app, money and dispute panels included, inside a change whose diff looks like it only adds tokens. Nothing in either file reads the new names yet; migrating existing ad hoc radii and breakpoints onto this scale is a separate, reviewed pass. Breakpoint values are listed for reference only, a CSS custom property cannot appear inside an `@media` condition, so consolidating an existing breakpoint onto this scale still means writing the literal px value.

**`docs/chat.js` had its own small, independent colour surface.** Its injected `<style>` string hardcoded `#07071A`, `#EEEEFF` and `#0d0d24` rather than reading the host page's own tokens; the first two already matched the current palette by value; all three are now `var(--bg)`, `var(--ink)` and `var(--panel2)` so a future palette change reaches this script the same way it reaches everything else. `#25D366` (the WhatsApp launcher) and the `700px` breakpoint (tuned specifically to stop the launcher overlapping form fields on `/jobs/new` and `/apply`) are unchanged, both confirmed still correct after the edit.

## 2026-09-03 · The homepage says what is live, and the ticker of invented events is gone

**`docs/index.html` is the homepage visitors see; `web/app/page.tsx` is a redirect to the portal and stays one.** A brief to "optimise the homepage" named the app file, but that file is twenty-six lines sending app.yaadly.co.uk to `/portal`, and its own comment records why: it carries the email-confirmation session through to sign-in, and a landing page at that address once stranded newly confirmed clients. The marketing homepage at yaadly.co.uk is the page every item in the brief describes, so that is the one that changed. Putting a second homepage on the app hostname is a separate decision with that risk attached; nobody has taken it.

**The hero promised "nobody is paid until you approve the evidence", and the strip under the doors cycled through invented events with fake timestamps, "08:04 · payment released · worker paid".** Neither was true. `docs/marketplace.html` says online payment is not yet live and the client pays the tradesperson directly against a signed agreement, and CLAUDE.md §9 lists payment integration as not built. A homepage that claims the December platform while selling the September service is the overclaim guardrail (§6 of the project brain) broken on the front door. The ticker is deleted, not toned down: fabricated activity is invented proof whatever the numbers say. The money promise now sits in a two-column block, "Live now" and "Planned", with the planned column saying in words what happens today. When holding goes live, move the line across; do not delete the column, because the next planned thing will need it.

**Three doors became two, owners and tradespeople.** All three old doors were client-side (post a job, book a service, business), so the worker's path was a footnote under a ticker. The Mirror Rule says a protection that only faces one side is not finished, and the same holds for the front page. Business is now one line under the doors with its own link; the `/business` page is unchanged. The hero carries one primary button, "Start a project", which goes to the same `/jobs/new` route as the nav's "Post a job"; the nav label was deliberately left alone because it is shared markup across every page and the app header, and renaming it is a site-wide change of its own. The secondary "Ask Yaadly" is a plain link to `/contact` that four lines of script upgrade into pressing the chat launcher chat.js already puts on the page, so a blocked script still lands somewhere useful. `.btn` is copied from `yaadly.css` rather than linked, because the homepage carries its own `:root` and the rest of that sheet would restyle the hero. The eyebrow now says where Yaadly is launching, Kingston and Portmore, instead of the company number, which the footer already carries.

**The cards were restyled the same day on founder feedback: "childish and animated".** The first cut kept the old door component, pill badges, 22px corners, a watermark icon in the corner, a rise-in on load and a lift-and-glow on hover. All of that is gone from both the doors and the status boxes. What remains is one 12px panel with a thin rule, a small mono overline in place of the pill, hairline rows with prices in tabular mono on the right, and a solid button for the action. Nothing moves on load and hover changes only the border. The hero's word animation was left as it was, because the feedback was about the boxes.

**Corrected by the founder the same evening: Yaadly does not oversee a job from start to finish.** The first copy said a named person "oversees the job from start to finish", which is not the service. What happens is a final review once the work is complete, before the client signs it off. Ongoing oversight exists only as the paid care and oversight service, priced on the services page. The hero, both link-preview descriptions, the tradespeople door and the status lead now say final review. Anyone writing homepage copy: the founder's own description of the service beats what the evidence flow could technically support.

**Second correction the same evening: the planned payment line described the wrong structure.** It said money would be "held safely with a licensed payment provider" and that today the client pays the tradesperson directly. Both came from `docs/marketplace.html` and `docs/payments.html`, and both are stale against the ledger entries of 3 September 2026: Yaadly is principal on every lane, the client buys the job from Yaadly and Yaadly pays the tradesperson, terms are agreed in writing per job with no fixed percentage stated anywhere, short jobs authorise the card and capture on approval, long jobs run on an invoice, and none of it takes a client's payment until legal sign-off and insurance are in hand. The homepage line now says exactly that and nothing more: you pay Yaadly, the tradesperson is not paid until you approve, terms per job, switched on after legal and insurance. "Your card is not charged until you approve" is deliberately absent, because the ledger says it is only true on the short-job route and must not leak onto invoice jobs. Lesson for the next session: on anything touching money, the ledger outranks the marketing pages, which lag it.

**Then moved from Planned to Live now, founder instruction: "that is the payment structure I am doing now."** The "switched on once legal sign-off and insurance are in hand" sentence came off with the move, because it was the only thing keeping the line in the planned column. `docs/payments.html` opened with "It is not switched on yet" and contradicted the homepage; the founder said that was no longer true and had it updated in the same session. Its first block now says the same three things as the homepage, you pay Yaadly, terms agreed in writing per job, tradesperson not paid until approval, and keeps the 15% and 12% fee paragraph in the present tense. The "never more than 25% up front" rule is gone, per the 3 September ledger entry that no fixed percentage is stated anywhere. The short-job card authorisation and long-job invoice mechanism from the same ledger entry is not on the page: the founder confirmed the structure, not that sentence, and "your card is not charged until you approve" may only ever appear on the short-job route.

**The brief for "optimise the /trades page" described a page that explains the worker opportunity: who it's for, what it costs, how to apply.** What actually sat at that route was the client-facing "browse open jobs by trade" board, linked once, from `/jobs` as "All 18 trades." Two different audiences, one URL. Asked Monique directly rather than guess: repurpose `/trades` for workers, and move the client board somewhere else. It moved to `/jobs/trades`, under the job board's own layout rather than a new top-level route, because it already reads open job counts off `open_jobs` and belongs with the rest of the marketplace. The `/jobs` link was updated to match; nothing else referenced the old path.

The new `/trades` page follows the signed, more recent document rather than the stale marketing line. ## 2026-09-03 · The worker directory and profile page stop reading worker_email and phone off the base tables

Found during a UX pass on `/jobs?tab=workers` and `/workers/[slug]`, not touching either yet: `worker_profiles.phone` and `.worker_email` sat on the same row as `name` and `trade`, and the two SELECT policies that made a profile publicly readable (`wp_select_public`, `wp_select_signed_in`) were row-level only, the way Postgres RLS always is. Row-level says which rows a role may see, never which columns, so anybody holding the publishable key could already `GET /rest/v1/worker_profiles?select=name,worker_email,phone` and read every active worker's contact details, no login and no app code involved. `worker_checks` and `portfolio` carried the identical shape of leak, `worker_email` present on every row purely as a join key nobody meant to publish. Confirmed live before writing the fix: 6 active profiles, 4 with a phone on file, 6 with an email, none of it ever rendered anywhere in the app.

**Column-level GRANT/REVOKE was considered and rejected as the whole fix.** Postgres supports revoking a column from a role independent of RLS, which would have stopped `anon` in one line. It does not stop a signed-in non-admin: `authenticated` is the same role a client or a browsing worker connects as, and `concierge.html` reads Postgres as `authenticated` too (the publishable key plus Monique's own session, `is_admin()` doing the separating per CLAUDE.md §6), so a column revoke broad enough to stop one signed-in stranger reading another worker's phone would have taken the desk's own visibility with it. Grants are not conditional on `is_admin()`; policies are.

**The fix actually made:** three views, the same shape `published_reviews` already established for `reviews.subject_email` → `subject_slug`: `public_worker_profiles`, `public_worker_checks`, `public_portfolio`, none carrying `worker_email` or `phone`, `worker_checks`/`portfolio` exposing `subject_slug` instead of the email so the app joins on the public slug rather than a private column. The three base tables lose their broad SELECT policies; `worker_profiles` keeps exactly one, `worker_email = auth.jwt() email OR worker_user = auth.uid() OR is_admin()`, covering the two existing call sites that legitimately need their own row (`web/app/portal/(gated)/worker/page.tsx` reading its own phone, `web/app/jobs/actions.ts` reading its own name for a quote) without opening anyone else's. `worker_checks`/`portfolio` keep only their pre-existing admin-only ALL policy, so the desk's write path is untouched. Migration: `supabase/migrations/20260903f_worker_profile_columns_stop_answering_the_open_internet.sql`; the app-side switch to the three views is in the same change (`web/app/jobs/page.tsx`, `web/app/jobs/new/page.tsx`, `web/app/workers/[slug]/page.tsx`).

**Written up before being applied to `leffyisvfvjwzilydlwf`.** The migration touches production RLS directly and the auto-mode classifier declined to run it without her sign-off; she asked to see the file first. If this paragraph is being read and `select viewname from pg_views where viewname like 'public_%'` comes back empty, the migration is still sitting in the repo, not live, and the directory/profile pages will render zero workers rather than error until it is applied. See the matching RUNBOOK.md entry for the verification queries.

## 2026-09-03 · The job form became six stages, and asking who holds the key switched on a gate that was never firing

**The form was three screens and one of the three had nothing on it to fill in.** It said "Saved" and "photos help" and had no field, so a person was charged a step for reading a paragraph. It is now six, one question each, in the order somebody actually thinks about a problem with a building: what is wrong, where it is, how soon, what you can show us, how to reach you, then the whole thing on one screen before it goes. Nothing personal is asked before stage five, which was already true and is now visible, because the top of the form says up front exactly what it will ask for and that it will not ask for an address, an account or a card. The stage rules moved to `web/lib/jobs/new-form.ts` and are tested; they used to be three boolean expressions inline in the JSX.

**The important part of this change is a column that was always null.** The form now asks who can let a worker in, and writes the answer to `jobs.access_type`. `enforce_vetted_worker_on_quote` (migration `20260831d`) reads that column: a worker still in Probation, meaning the police check and the telephoned references have not cleared, is refused a job where they would hold keys or work inside an occupied home. Because this form had never filled it, the gate was skipped on every job posted from the web since the day it was written. The words were on the page and the door was open, which is the same sentence that migration used about itself. Founder decision to switch it on, 3 Sep 2026, taken as a permissions change rather than a copy change, because that is what it is: a worker who could quote a web job yesterday may be refused one today, and the refusal is correct.

**Which makes four sentences of page copy load bearing, so they are tested as code.** The gate matches on `/(key|keys)/i` and `/(on site|occupied|lives there|at home)/i`. "A key is held locally, by family or a neighbour" blocks a probation worker because of one word in it; tidy that word away and the gate opens with nothing going red. `web/tests/new-job-form.test.mjs` asserts the expected verdict for each of the four options, one test each, naming the option, plus the two patterns character for character against the migration. It also asserts that no option says a house is empty, because `open_jobs` is granted to `anon` and selects `access_type`, so those sentences are readable by anybody who opens the marketplace. Carrying the signal a worker needs without publishing that nobody is home is the reason they are phrased the way they are rather than the more obvious way round.

**Urgency was asked for the first time, in wording that was already settled.** `jobs.urgency` exists, the board prints it and the desk has a column for it that read "not said" on every job that came this way. The three options are the ones from `preview/index.html`, which is where product decisions get made before they are built, and the first one has to keep the word "urgent" in it because the desk chips urgency red on `/urgent|emergency/i`. That is asserted too. Required rather than optional, because an optional question on a form is a question most people skip, and a job with no urgency is a job that needs a phone call before anybody can quote it.

**What was NOT asked, and this is deliberate.** No street address, and no name or number for whoever holds the key. `yaad-post-job` accepts both and the prototype asks for both. Parish is what matching and the board actually use, `yaad-inbound` says so in its own prompt, and a third person's name and telephone number is personal data collected from somebody who has not yet given us their own. So the access question is four non-identifying choices, and the details get picked up in the reply.

**The draft promise was half true and is now whole.** The job was already written to Postgres before any personal detail, which was right, and the comment at the top of the component said so. What it did not say is that the reference and the portal code lived in React state and nowhere else, so closing the tab left a job on file that its own client could never reach again and lost them the code that opens it. That is worse than no draft because it reads as covered. The answers and the reference are now kept in the browser for seven days under one key, so a returning visitor lands on the stage they stopped at and keeps working on the same job rather than making a second one. The name, the contact detail and the portal code are deliberately not stored: the first two because this form is filled in on shared family phones, the third because job id plus code is a credential, and the code comes back from the server on the next save anyway.

**A failed save stopped being a locked door.** The throttle on `yaad-post-job` is eight creates per caller per hour and it can trip on a shared connection. The old form simply would not advance. It now shows the function's own words and offers both Try again and Carry on without saving, and a job that carries on reaches Monique through the enquiry with no reference number on it, which is how every job arrived before drafts existed. Losing a reference number is a nuisance. Losing six sentences about somebody's mother's roof is not.

**No file input, and that is the decision rather than the omission.** `yaad-post-job` still has a photos array left over from the deleted `docs/index.html` funnel: up to eight images, **no size limit at all**, written as base64 into `evidence`, which is immutable, with the GPS coordinate the phone wrote into the file left on them. Nothing has called it since that funnel was deleted, and reviving it would have been the easy way to tick "improve uploads". The portal's own route, `job_photos`, is size capped, goes to private storage, strips the location and can be deleted by the person who sent it. So stage four is a real stage that says what to photograph, and the confirmation screen hands over `/portal/join?job=…&code=…&next=photos`, which sets the account up and lands directly on the photo panel instead of on a dashboard with the upload folded away three clicks deep. `next=photos` is honoured for that one value only and only alongside a job, so WhatsApp arrivals still land on `/portal` exactly as before. Whether an anonymous form should ever accept a file at all is a data protection question and is Monique's, not this change's.

## 2026-09-03 · The rest of the audit: one money formatter, two CI gaps, a throttled question box, and the accessibility work

**Working through the medium and low findings. Also, and more usefully, finding out how many of them were wrong.**

**Ten money formatters became one.** Not nine, as the audit said: the services page had a tenth for GBP. Seven passed `en-JM` and three passed `en-US`, which sounds like the defect and is not, because for a J$ amount both locales group identically and the locale never showed. What did show is that seven rounded and three did not, so one job could read J$1,234,567.89 in one panel and J$1,234,568 in another, on the same screen, on the product whose premise is that the numbers can be trusted. `lib/money.ts` has four exports because the call sites genuinely need three null behaviours (`null` out, `""` out, and never-null) plus the multi-currency invoice case, and collapsing them would only push the null handling back into the components. Rounding won because Jamaican pricing is quoted in whole dollars. 15 tests.

**`amount()` stopped printing a lone em dash for a missing total.** It now says "not set". A dash in a money column is ambiguous between nothing and zero, and those are very different answers to somebody asking what they owe. It also happened to be one of the few em dashes left in user-facing output.

**CI grew two jobs, and one of them contradicts a comment I had to think about first.** The workflow said `next build` was deliberately skipped because `check-env.mjs` blocks a build without Supabase values and the workflow has no reason to hold secrets. The first half is true and the conclusion was not: `check-env` only asks that the variables are PRESENT, the artifact is discarded, and no page queries Supabase at build time (every data route is dynamic; only `/`, `/_not-found` and `/icon.svg` prerender). Verified by building locally with `env -i` and placeholders before touching CI. It earns its place because `tsc --noEmit` does not generate Next's route types and `next build` does, so a route-shape mistake passes typecheck and fails the deploy. That gap was open earlier the same day, when a deleted layout left a dangling reference in a generated validator.

**The taxonomy drift check is the same idea as the `_shared` one, for the list the marketplace is built on.** Four copies: the generated source, a byte copy the marketing site loads, an inlined copy in the prototype, and a TypeScript restatement the app imports because it cannot import a file that declares `var`. They agreed and nothing kept them agreeing. The only reason a client's roofing job and a worker's roofing profile find each other is that both came from one list; the day they diverge, both halves stay individually valid and nothing notices. The check was proved by adding a nineteenth trade to the app copy and watching it fail with both lists printed, then reverting.

**The question box is throttled in Postgres, not in the page.** `/ask` was the only unthrottled public write in the application, and `area` was capped by a `maxLength` attribute and nothing else, which is not a control. `ask_question()` now does the length floor, both caps, the throttle and `published = false`, and the caller cannot set `published` because the function never reads a value for it. Ten an hour per caller: generous for a human, useless for a script. Applied in two phases on purpose, the function first and the `anyone may ask` policy dropped only after the web deploy, because the deployed action still does a direct insert until the new build is live and dropping the policy first would break `/ask` for the length of a deploy. Caller key is a truncated SHA-256 of the address, the same shape and the same reasoning as `post_job_attempts`: a rate limit must not quietly become the one place this business keeps personal data nobody asked it to keep.

**`search_path` is now pinned on every function in the schema, and the advisor's framing was worth checking.** All nine remaining were SECURITY INVOKER, so they run with the caller's own privileges and there is nothing to escalate to; somebody bending the path for one of those is attacking themselves. They are pinned anyway for a duller and more real reason: four are trigger functions, a trigger fires in whatever session context is writing, and `sync_job_status` in particular decides what stage a job is at. A trigger resolving `jobs` to something other than `public.jobs` would corrupt data quietly rather than fail loudly. Applied with `ALTER FUNCTION`, which sets one attribute and retypes no bodies, so the migration cannot change behaviour by transcription error.

**The chip groups are the accessibility fix that mattered most.** Eighteen trades and fourteen parishes on `/jobs/new` and `/apply` were bare buttons in a plain div: a screen reader heard eighteen unrelated buttons, could not tell which was chosen, and never heard that the group was required. They are now named groups with `aria-pressed` on each control. `aria-pressed` rather than a radiogroup because these really are toggles, and tapping the chosen one clears it, which a radio cannot do. The JoinFlow buttons were also missing `type="button"`.

**The approve button announces its outcome now, and the existing reasoning was right.** Its comment explains there is no visible success message because the ledger re-rendering IS the confirmation and a second visible claim could go stale. That holds for somebody who can see the ledger move. A screen reader user got nothing: the label went from "Approving…" back to "Approve this stage", indistinguishable from the tap doing nothing, on the one control in this product that moves money. So the confirmation is announced off-screen rather than shown. It is the non-visual counterpart to the ledger advancing, not a competing claim, and it cannot go stale because it is written once when the action returns.

**A correction to the audit, which matters more than any single fix.** Five of its fine-grained per-route claims were wrong, all in the same direction, all found by opening the file before changing it:

| The audit said | Actually |
|---|---|
| sign-in has no live region on its error | It has `role="alert"` |
| sign-in has no route to `/portal/join` | It links there, in its own footer |
| `/portal/join` has no live region | It has `role="alert"` |
| the approve button announces nothing | It announces errors; only success was silent |
| the guidelines page shows no version or date | It prints "Version 1.3 · date" above each document |

The audit's structural findings held up, and every one of the four high-risk items was real and is closed. The per-route detail did not hold up as well, and the pattern is clear: those claims came from reading a page's rendered output and its main render path, not from grepping the file for the attribute in question. An audit that cries wolf is one nobody reads, which is the reasoning already written into this repo's own secrets scanner. The published audit has been corrected rather than quietly amended.

**What was deliberately not done, and why.** The `/jobs` statistics render as em dashes by an explicit founder decision recorded in the code ("keep the row, leave the figures blank until there are real ones"), so it is not mine to change. The payment claim splitting by path is a founder decision about what the business says. The "Ask a Yaad" and "Ask Yaadly" naming is brand. A full CSP needs nonces threaded through the document and its own change, report-only first, exactly as `next.config.ts` already argues. `npm run lint` still waits on the one pre-existing error being fixed in its own commit, per `CLAUDE.md`. Splitting the 1,766 line JoinFlow and the 1,407 line job room by role are refactors that deserve their own change and their own testing. Reconciling the two service stage vocabularies touches what a client is told about how far along their money is, and is not a thing to do quickly at the end of a long session.

## 2026-09-03 · The question form keeps what you typed, and the desk got somewhere to publish from

**Written after the fact, because the write path underneath this changed while it was being built.** The finding that started it: `/ask` had never saved a question and told every visitor "Received" anyway. `questions` had row level security and no insert policy for anon, so every question typed there was refused with 42501 while the action ignored the error and redirected to `?sent=1`. The table held nil rows and that was the reason. The interim fix was an insert policy, `anyone may ask`, and that policy is the `20260903d3` in the migrations folder: it is now dropped, because the throttled `ask_question()` is the only door and `20260903j` explains why that is the better answer. **The lesson worth keeping is not the policy, it is that a false receipt is worse than a visible failure.** A visible failure gets reported. This one left people believing they had asked, and waiting.

**What is left here is the form, and it stopped using the URL to say what happened.** Outcomes arrived as `/ask?sent=1`, `?sent=throttled`, `?sent=short`, `?sent=error`. Every one of those still exists with the same words; they are returned by the action and rendered in place. A URL flag cannot survive a refusal without losing the box, so a refused question came back empty and somebody had to type it again, and a refresh or a shared link redrew a receipt for a question nobody had asked. The result now carries the text back, the button says "Sending your question" and is disabled for the round trip so a second tap cannot duplicate, errors land in a live region and mark the field, and the receipt dies with the page.

**A phone number is refused, a price is not, and the two are genuinely hard to tell apart.** A bare run of seven digits is a Jamaican local number and it is also 1500000 in Jamaican dollars, which is the archetypal question this board exists to answer. The rule has to choose which way to be wrong, so it refuses what cannot be a price, meaning runs long enough for an area code, anything after a plus, and digits grouped the way a phone number is grouped rather than the way money is. The ambiguous bare run goes through and the desk marks it for a second read. Blocking a pricing question fails the exact people the board is for, on every honest submission, with a message they cannot act on because their number **is** the question. Publishing a stray number needs both a visitor typing it and the person at the desk missing it. The module also refuses at 500 characters rather than letting `ask_question()` truncate silently, because cutting the end off somebody's question without saying so is the worse of the two.

**The desk got a Questions view, because a moderation gate with nowhere to stand is not a gate.** Publishing was previously a row edit in the Supabase dashboard, which meant it would not happen. Ordinary registry work: publish, take down, the consequence spelled out on the button, and a tile and alert on the Overview so a waiting question shows on the first screen rather than needing to be looked for.

**No response time is promised, and that is a decision rather than a gap.** `/jobs/new` says one working day because the founder defined one. Nothing defines a timing for a public question that waits on a stranger to answer it, so the page says there is none and to check back. Inventing one would have been the easiest sentence in the rewrite and the only untrue one.


---

## 2026-09-03 · Four audit findings closed: the RPC grants, the Twilio door, the retired password routes, and the first tests on the trust logic

**All four came out of a read-only audit of the whole application on the same day. None is a feature. Each one is something that was already true and should not have been.**

**PostgREST publishes anything the caller's role may execute, and Supabase grants that by default.** Every function in the `public` schema is reachable at `/rest/v1/rpc/<name>` by any role holding EXECUTE, and Supabase's default privileges hand EXECUTE to `anon` and `authenticated` on creation. So a function is on the open internet unless somebody remembers to take it off. Two were both exposed and unguarded: `_do_agree_kickoff_pack(pack, side, email)` takes the side and the email as arguments and writes the agreement row, so anyone holding a pack id could file both sides and flip `both_confirmed_at`; `agree_quote_via_whatsapp(job, phone)` authenticates by matching a phone number supplied in the request body against `jobs.client_phone`, which is safe when Twilio is the caller and has already proved the sender, and is authentication by assertion when the caller is anybody with curl. The dual agreement on a Kickoff Pack is one of the two things this product sells and it was reachable from a browser address bar.

**The revoke is scoped by evidence, not by category.** `authenticated` keeps EXECUTE on everything the concierge desk calls, because the desk is a static page reading Postgres with the publishable key: Monique signed in **is** `authenticated`, and `is_admin()` is the only thing separating her from everybody else. Revoking `authenticated` on `raise_job_agency_fee_invoice`, `raise_service_invoice` and `release_materials_tranche` would have taken the desk's invoicing and materials release offline. `service_role` keeps everything, because that is how the Edge Functions call them. Only the three that no browser has any business calling lost both. `approve_stage_via_whatsapp`, `choose_worker_via_whatsapp` and `relay_confirmed_report` already had exactly this treatment, so this is that same pattern applied to the ones that were missed rather than a new idea.

**One function's grant came through PUBLIC, and revoking `anon` alone would have been a no-op on it.** `release_materials_tranche` carried a bare `=X/postgres` in its ACL, which is the grant to PUBLIC, and PUBLIC covers `anon` regardless of what `anon` itself holds. Every REVOKE in the migration therefore names `public` as well, and every desk function is re-granted to `authenticated` explicitly afterwards rather than left to survive, because on that one function the desk's access was partly coming through the grant being removed.

**The WhatsApp door now fails closed, and the fix is at the call site, not in the signature module.** `checkTwilioSignature()` reports `{ok:true, checked:false}` when no `TWILIO_AUTH_TOKEN` was passed in. That stays exactly as it is: it is a statement of fact about what happened, it is what lets the module be tested against a throwaway secret, and its own test asserts it. What was missing was anybody deciding what to **do** about that fact, and `yaad-inbound` did nothing, so an unsigned request walked in. That was proportionate when the worst case was a junk enquiry row. It is not now: this endpoint runs with `--no-verify-jwt`, the signature is its only door, and behind it the function can call `agree_quote_via_whatsapp`, `agree_kickoff_pack_via_whatsapp`, `choose_worker_via_whatsapp` and `approve_stage_via_whatsapp`, the last of which fires `raise_worker_pay_invoice_on_stage_approval`. The worst case today is a forged stage approval that raises a worker pay invoice. `index.ts` now refuses a Twilio request it could not verify, with 503 rather than 403 because it is our misconfiguration and not the sender's bad signature. Scoped to `isTwilio`: the web chat also reports `checked:false` and must keep working, because its door is the origin allowlist in `web-chat.ts`. **Mechanism reports, policy decides.** The token is set in production, so this changes nothing for live traffic; it changes what happens on the day it goes missing, which used to be silent.

**The password routes forward instead of being deleted.** Sign in has been passwordless since 31 August and says so in its own comment, but `/portal/reset` still rendered two password fields and called `updateUser({ password })`, making it the only thing in the application that could mint a credential nothing accepts, and `/portal/forgot` still described the mechanism. Neither was linked from anywhere; both were reachable by URL. They are not deleted, because a recovery link may be sitting in an inbox and a 404 is a worse answer than a working one. `/portal/forgot` redirects on the server. `/portal/reset` must forward **in the browser** and carry the hash across by hand: GoTrue returns the session in the URL fragment, a fragment is never sent to the server, and a server redirect would throw the session away and land somebody on a sign-in form holding credentials that had just been discarded. That is the exact failure the site root had before it was fixed and it was not worth repeating one route down. **Left for the founder:** the Supabase Auth email templates may still contain a password-reset template pointing at `/portal/reset`. Retiring it changes what clients receive, so it is hers. Until then both routes forward and nobody lands anywhere broken.

**The trust logic has tests now, and getting there needed a harness fix.** `gates.ts` decides what a client must do before a tradesperson can see their job; `journey.ts` decides where a job sits on the rail a client approves against. Both are pure, both are read by two screens each, and neither had a single test. `gates.ts` could not even be imported: it reads `CG_VERSION` from `lib/legal-copy.json`, TypeScript resolves that with `resolveJsonModule` and no import attribute, and plain Node refuses the module with `ERR_IMPORT_ATTRIBUTE_MISSING` before an assertion can run. A `load` hook in `tests/ts-resolve-hooks.mjs` supplies the attribute, so the shipped file stays exactly as Next wants it. 58 new tests, 47 to 105. The assertions deliberately mirror what Postgres enforces, so if one has to change to make a test pass, the checklist and the database have stopped agreeing and a client is being told "nothing outstanding" while the database still refuses to open their job.

## 2026-09-03 · The three text tiers, and a status pill that means something

**Two findings from a full read of every route, both measured rather than felt.**

**The third text tier was not readable.** Against the page background `#07071A`, the palette measured: `--ink` 17.4:1, `--mute` 4.79:1, `--dim` 2.04:1 on the app and 2.16:1 on the marketing site. WCAG AA wants 4.5:1 for normal text, so `--dim` was not failing narrowly, it was at less than half the floor. It is used 269 times across 60 files in `web/` alone, and not for decoration: field labels, job references, "nothing held right now", "paid off-platform within 3 working days of release", "we are working in Kingston and Portmore first". That is the copy the product is trusted on, set at a contrast a tradesperson on a phone in daylight cannot read. Trust copy nobody can read builds no trust, so this was the highest-value change on the list and the cheapest.

**The palette only had room for two readable tiers, so both moved.** `--mute` went `#7878A8` to `#9E9ECB` (4.79:1 to 7.78:1) and `--dim` went to `#7C7CA6` (5.02:1), which leaves a 1.55:1 step between them, enough to still read as two tiers. Lifting only `--dim` would have collapsed it into `--mute`; leaving `--mute` alone and finding room underneath it was arithmetically impossible. The values live in **six places** and must move together: `web/app/globals.css`, `docs/yaadly.css`, and the inline `:root` blocks in `docs/index.html`, `docs/marketplace.html`, `docs/business.html` and `docs/services.html`. The four inline blocks are the residue of the same copy-paste the 2 Sep header entry below describes; the stylesheet edit alone reached exactly half the site, which is how the split was found. The light-section tokens (`--l-*`) are a separate scale and were not touched.

**Every job status looked the same, so none of them said anything.** "Closed", "Draft, not live yet", "Quotes in, waiting on you" and "Evidence waiting on you" all rendered in one neutral purple pill. A client with nine jobs had to read all nine to find the one holding them up. `components/portal/statusTone.ts` now holds four tones and their colours, shared by the job rows and the worker's invoice rows: **gold** waiting on the person reading it, **purple** moving and not on them, **green** finished and paid, **grey** not live yet. All four clear AA on the panel background. Tone is deliberately per audience and not per status, which is why the two portals still pass their own maps: "Open for quotes" is `moving` for a client who can only wait and `waiting` for a worker who can quote it right now. On the founder's own portal this turns nine identical pills into one gold one.

**"Stage 0" came out of the job row.** It is the rail's internal counter, it means nothing to the person reading it, and on a job that has not started it prints "0", which reads as a failure rather than a beginning. The pill above it already says where the job is in words. The row now shows when the job last moved, which was fetched to sort by and never displayed.

**Nothing about money, access or business logic moved.** No route added or removed, no query changed, no server action touched, no RLS policy touched. `--dim` had no non-text uses to break.

## 2026-09-02 · One header, one file, instead of eight copies of it

**Founder's own report, and she was right about the cause even without reading the code: "when I click on Marketplace or I click on Jobs it changes the menu to a different menu, it looks like a glitch."** The header was copy-pasted into all eight marketing pages, and each page then carried its own CSS for it. The four newest pages (index, marketplace, services, business) each had an inline block; they disagreed on the bar colour (0.85 against 0.88 alpha), the container width (1080, 1060, 1100), the active-tab treatment (a purple pill on two pages, plain bold on one, no rule at all on the fourth) and the width at which the menu vanished entirely (760, 860, 1024). The four older pages (contact, prices, faq, payments) used `nav.top` in `yaadly.css`, which still painted the pre-rebrand green-black bar and bright white tab labels. The app had a fifth header again, with a shorter menu: "Marketplace" where the site said "Overview", and no "Job board" tab at all. Nothing was broken in the sense of a bug report; it just redrew itself on every click, which is what a person reads as a glitch.

**`docs/nav.css` is now the only place the header is defined**, and all eight pages link it. Every rule is scoped under `.site-nav`, which is what lets a page that carries its own complete design system inline link one shared file without anything else on it moving. The nav block came out of `yaadly.css` and out of the four inline blocks; a comment sits where each used to be. The markup is byte-identical on all eight pages, only the `on` class moves. `web/components/SiteNav.tsx` carries the same six tabs in the same order with the same measurements written in Tailwind, because the app does not load the marketing stylesheet.

**The menu now has the same eleven things everywhere**: Overview, Job board, Managed services, For business, Contact, Book a call, then the three portal links, then Post a job. Overview and Job board are the pair that used to be confused for each other: Overview is the story page and lives on the marketing site, Job board is the live board and lives in the app, so crossing between them is the only cross-domain hop in the row and it now happens in the same tab rather than spawning a new one into a header that looked different again.

**The sizes in `nav.css` were measured against the real font, not chosen.** With IBM Plex loaded, brand plus six tabs plus three portal links plus the button come to exactly the 1100px the page content uses; before the trim they overflowed the page grid by 86px and the button dropped onto a second row on the app. Growing any padding or label in that row pushes the button back out past the grid on a wide screen. Signed in, the three portal links come out of the row and the email and Sign out take their place, which is both the space and the right content: a signed-in tradesperson has no use for "Join as a pro".

**A seventh tab, "How we use AI", 6 September 2026, on Monique's instruction.** The page had existed since 5 September and was linked from every footer and from the business page, and nothing in the header, which is where a visitor actually looks. It sits between "For business" and "Contact" on all twelve marketing pages and in `SiteNav.tsx`, so the row still reads as one site across the two hostnames. Twelve, not nine: the three legal pages (cancellation, privacy, terms) were written after the 2 September header work with the old `nav.top` markup and no link to `nav.css`, so on the live site they had been showing a row of bare underlined links with no bar and no "Post a job" button since they were published. Found while adding the tab, fixed in the same change on Monique's instruction: they now carry the byte-identical header block and link `nav.css` like every other page. The dark bar over their cream sections is the same treatment `faq.html` already had. Every page now asks for `nav.css?v=2`, because the row changed width and a browser holding the old file would draw the button off the edge. It did not fit: measured on the live site with IBM Plex loaded, the tab is 105px and the row overflowed by 65px, which is the paragraph above coming true. Two ways to make room were measured rather than chosen. Trimming every padding and gap in the row recovered 50px and left "Post a job" sitting inside the row's own padding, off the grid by 15px. Widening the row to 1180px fits it with 15px to spare, the header sitting 40px proud of the 1100px content grid on each side, which was judged less visible than a cramped row. The portal links now hide at 1180px rather than 1080px, because between those two widths the row is narrower than its contents and the button dropped. Both numbers are the same in `nav.css` and `SiteNav.tsx`. An eighth tab means something comes out; the row has no slack left to measure.

**Narrow screens now keep the menu.** Every old version hid `.views` and `.quiet-links` outright below its own breakpoint, which left a phone with a logo and nothing to navigate by. The portal links drop below 1080px; below 820px the tabs move to their own full-width row and scroll sideways, so every page stays reachable on a phone.

## 2026-09-02 · A place in the worker portal to see the actual invoices, not only the computed estimate

**Founder's own instruction, direct continuation of the same session's work: "there should be a place in the portal where they can review the money of jobs and what is pending."** `WorkerMoneyPanel` (Stage 5.6, still in place, unchanged) has always shown one computed figure per job, 88% of labour plus materials, held or released off whether the whole job is `complete`. It was never the real document trail, and now that `invoices.worker_email` exists (this session, same day) there was finally something real to show instead of a client-side estimate.

**`WorkerInvoices`, a new section beneath the existing money panel, not a replacement for it.** Lists the worker's own actual invoices, job by job, stage by stage: the real `period_label`, the real `total_pence`, a **Pending** badge on anything `sent` that the worker hasn't yet recorded receiving payment for (`jobs.pay_method`, the existing self-report). Deliberately worded in the panel's own copy that "pending" is not proof money moved, only the worker's own later note against the job says that, the same honesty `CLAUDE.md 9` already requires of the rest of this portal.

**A real display gap found and fixed before it shipped, not after.** The first version looked up a job's title from the same `jobs` array the page already had, scoped to this worker's *live* stake (`jobs.worker_email` or an active quote). An invoice's own `job_id` isn't guaranteed to fall inside that narrower list, an older or differently-seeded row can name a job this worker no longer shows as "live" but was still genuinely invoiced against, and the first live test showed exactly that: "Untitled job" on a real, correctly-scoped invoice. Fixed by looking up any missing titles directly, by id, rather than assuming the page's existing job list was exhaustive.

**A second parallel-session hazard, same class as before, caught before anything was lost this time.** Mid-edit, `git status` showed `On branch main` again, silently, with none of this session's other work in sight, meaning both new files had just been written against `main`'s own base rather than `the-approve-button`'s. Nothing was committed yet, so nothing was actually at risk, caught by checking the branch before committing rather than after, per the runbook entry the last time this happened. Resolved with `git stash`, a clean `checkout` back to `the-approve-button`, and `stash pop`, re-verified with a typecheck and a live reload before writing anything further.

**Verified live**: the panel renders three real invoices across two jobs correctly, two still `draft` (raised before this session's auto-send fix existed, a real and accurate distinction, not a display bug), one `sent` and correctly marked Pending, all under their correct job titles after the lookup fix.

## 2026-09-02 · A worker can read their own pay invoice; `invoices` had no column naming them at all

**Founder's own instruction: "a worker needs to know which job they are being paid for."** Checked before building anything, not assumed: `invoices` had `client_email`/`client_user` and nothing naming the worker at all. The worker portal's own money panel already shows a job's title per row, but that figure was always computed client-side from `job_quotes`/`jobs.status`, never tied to the actual invoice document raised in the worker's name; there was no RLS path by which a worker could read that document even if they knew its id.

**`invoices.worker_email`, set only on a `payable_to = 'worker'` row**, null on every other invoice type unchanged. `invoices_worker_read`/`lines_worker_read` mirror the existing client-read policies exactly: never a draft, only their own, matched on the same `lower(email) = lower(auth.jwt() ->> 'email')` shape used everywhere else in this codebase. Both raise functions now write it from the accepted quote's own `worker_email` at the moment they raise. Every worker-pay invoice already on file, raised earlier in this same session, was backfilled in the same migration, so nothing already sent sits invisible to the person it was always for.

**Verified live**: the four already-raised worker-pay invoices all backfilled correctly. A fresh trigger-fired raise, in a rolled-back transaction, wrote `worker_email` on insert without being told to separately. `pg_policies` confirms `invoices_worker_read`'s predicate matches what was written, not assumed from the migration text.

**A parallel-session hazard, caught before it did damage, not after.** Mid-session, `HEAD` in this shared working tree moved to `main` without this session doing it, twice, the second time silently: reading `DECISIONS.md` to add this very entry returned a much older version with none of today's prior entries in it, because `main`'s own copy of the file predates this branch's work entirely. Caught by noticing the missing entries were missing rather than assuming the read was current, confirmed with `git branch --show-current` and `git status`, and resolved by checking out `the-approve-button` again before writing anything, not by editing what was on screen. Nothing was lost: all of this session's commits were made while `HEAD` genuinely was on `the-approve-button` each time, confirmed from each commit's own branch-prefixed output, `main` moving under the working tree never touched them. See `RUNBOOK.md` for what to check if this happens again.

## 2026-09-02 · The invoice is sent the instant the client approves, no separate admin click; a real ordering bug caught on the first live test

**Refused first, then resolved, not overridden.** Asked to auto-send, this session refused on the governing rule ("a named human confirms every consequential step") and proposed a bulk-send button instead, since every other invoice send in this codebase runs through `yaad-invoice`'s `isAdmin()` gate and no admin session exists at the moment a trigger fires. The founder's reply resolved it rather than overruling it: "the client is approving the workflow." That is the named human the rule asks for, present, authenticated, taking a real action, it simply is not an admin, and this was never an admin's decision, it is arithmetic on terms already agreed. Confirmed against the actual database rather than assumed: `invoice_status_guard` only calls `is_admin()` on a `sent -> paid` transition, never on `draft -> sent`, so moving straight to `sent` inside the same security-definer chain the client's own `approve_stage()` already runs in needed no service-role bypass of anything, `yaad-invoice`'s own admin gate was never touched. Deliberately narrow: only the per-stage worker-pay invoice works this way. The whole-job fallback and every other invoice type in this codebase still need an admin's own click, unchanged.

**A real bug, caught on the very first live test, not by inspection.** The first version inserted the invoice with `status = 'sent'` directly, then inserted its lines as a second statement, and `invoice_line_price_guard` correctly refused: "a sent invoice's lines are frozen." Postgres rolled the whole function back on the error, so nothing broken reached the table, checked directly by counting every worker-pay invoice's own lines before writing the fix, not assumed from the error message alone. Corrected order: insert as `draft` (lines can only ever be added to a draft), insert the lines, only then update to `sent`, passing through `invoice_status_guard`'s ordinary `draft -> sent` branch exactly like any other invoice, `sent_at` included.

**Verified live, both the fix and the trigger together**: `raise_job_stage_worker_pay_invoice('JOB-TEST-KICKOFF-1', 2)` raised `INV-2026-0016`, J$3,900 (60% of J$6,500 labour), `status = 'sent'`, `sent_at` set, one correctly priced line. The trigger itself, fired by a real `stage_approvals` insert, in a rolled-back transaction: J$1,300 for stage 1 (20%), `status = 'sent'` with no separate step at all.
