Rather than a second pack pipeline, kickoff_drafts and kickoff_packs each gained a nullable `service_id` (`20260902q`), the pack read policy gained a service-client arm, and `yaad-kickoff-check` gained a services phase mirroring the quote phase: draft for every non-cancelled booking, auto-issue when guardrail-clean, hold dirty drafts for a human. The worker pack-ready trigger now fires only when `job_id` is present, because its notification names a job and a worker and a service pack has neither. The portal serves the pack read-only at `/portal/services/<id>/pack`, reusing the job pack's renderers; there is no dual confirmation on a service pack because Yaadly itself is the provider, and her "Confirm the work" click is the provider's signature. Editing: `edit_kickoff_doc_section()` rewrites one section's prose (cover, scope summary, timeline basis, payment note) and rides the existing `kickoff_touch` revision machinery, so an edit archives the old revision and de-approves the pack; `approve_kickoff_pack()` is the named-human way back. These two RPCs are the first editing and approving surface any pack has had, job packs included.

## The web can book a service directly, held (2 Sep 2026)

Founder reversed the enquiry-only door the same day it shipped: "should also be able to book directly on the web." The reconciliation is the held state, which was the real bandwidth gate all along: `yaad-book-service` creates a `held` booking priced from the catalogue, the receipt fires from the state change, and nothing proceeds until her confirm. The page's founding-rate figures are not in service_catalogue, so the clicked label (with its price) is recorded in notes for her to reconcile at confirm; that pricing question is hers, deliberately not solved in code. Technical Sign-off is not bookable because no catalogue row exists for it.

## The bot stands down after a handoff, and Monique replies through the same number (2 Sep 2026)

Two halves of one promise. First, `intake_threads.human_handling`: `wants_human` was one model call's read of one message and nothing remembered it, so a client who asked for a person got the bot again on their next message, which is a phone tree wearing a nicer sentence. The flag is set by `yaad-inbound` the moment a thread hands over and by `yaad-desk-reply` the moment Monique speaks on one, and while it is true the function keeps the record (transcript, photos, a high-priority push per message) but never runs the model. Deliberately no timeout and no automatic clearing: a bot rejoining a live human conversation because a clock ran out is worse than a quiet thread, and the per-message pushes are what stop a forgotten one being lost. Only the desk's "Hand back to the assistant" button clears it. Second, `yaad-desk-reply`: her reply used to come from her personal WhatsApp, arriving as a new chat from a strange number. Now the desk sends her words through the same Twilio sender the assistant uses, so the conversation continues where it started. That function deliberately contains no model call and does not screen her text through `guardrails.ts`: the banned-language screen exists to stop AI output making promises no human approved, and this is a named human speaking for herself, signed in and checked by `is_admin()` on the server side, with every database write under her own token so RLS is the access control. WhatsApp's 24 hour window is surfaced honestly as a refusal rather than worked around with a template, because a templated sentence pretending to be her defeats the point of the lane.

## The founder prices her own invoices; the AI still cannot (2 Sep 2026)

Her instruction: "make it so I can edit the invoices, just put whatever price, and add invoices at what I want it to be." The mechanism already existed unused: `price_source = 'manual'` has meant a human-set amount since the first invoicing migration, and the price guard refuses it only on AI-drafted invoices. `20260902s` and the desk changes open the doors without touching the rule: Confirm the work takes a price choice (full, founding, or a typed figure), the line editor gained a "my figure" tier with a typed amount, and a deliberate "Make it mine to price" click converts an AI draft to a human one before manual pricing unlocks, so owning the number is always a recorded human decision. Catalogue tiers stay guard-overwritten, so a typo against the service list still cannot ship. Technical Sign-off was added to the catalogue at the page's own £245 (no founding discount printed, so both tiers match) making it bookable through the web form; whether its founding rate should differ is her pricing call, editable in `service_catalogue`.

## The website chat is the WhatsApp assistant with a second door, and reaching a person is WhatsApp (2 Sep 2026)

Founder's ask: a chat on the site that answers questions there and connects to her if there is a problem; her rider in the same breath, "just use the whatsapp for now". Three shapes were on the table: a plain live-chat widget with no model, a third-party AI widget, or our own. A third-party AI widget was ruled out on the spot: it is a new processor of personal data, it does not obey `guardrails.ts`, and it will quote a price. Our own was built, but as a lane inside `yaad-inbound` rather than a new function, because "same brain" is only true if it is literally the same code: one prompt, one `readTheJob`, one `stripPromises`, one handoff counter, one screen. `docs/chat.js` posts `{channel: "web", visitor, text}` and gets JSON back where Twilio gets TwiML. A web thread is an `intake_threads` row keyed by a widget-minted hex token where the phone number would be, so it lands in the desk beside everything else with a draft job behind it, and nothing new needed RLS beyond the throttle table (`20260902t`, RLS on, no policies, service role only). Authentication on the door is the same posture as `yaad-enquiry`: Origin allowlist, token shape, and a three-way throttle, because every message is a paid model call and the door is public. The handoff copy differs by door on purpose: WhatsApp says "on this number", the web says "carry on on WhatsApp", because there is no reply lane back to a widget and a promise the desk cannot keep is worse than none. The piece that makes "you will not have to say it twice" true is the adoption step: a WhatsApp message carrying a `JOB-WEB-` reference (tolerant of a digit out, after her first live test typed one short) adopts the web thread onto that number, carries the transcript, keeps the job code and fills in the now-proven phone. It first marked the thread with Monique; she reversed that on watching it live ("AI should try help in the whatsapp chat before get passed to me"), so the assistant now continues on WhatsApp with the whole web conversation in front of it and a fresh three-turn allowance, handing over by the same rules as any thread, unless she had already personally replied in the web chat, in which case it stays hers. Deliberately not changed: the model provider; the chat runs on whatever `textmodel.ts` picks, which today is MiniMax, and a public page sending anonymous visitors' text there is the data protection question already recorded in this file, sharper now, and hers to time.

**Three additions the same evening, on her next two messages.** First, the reply lane after all ("a way I can answer back the person in the chat if they require a human"): `yaad-desk-reply` learned the web channel, writing her words to `web_chat_replies` (`20260902u`, RLS on, admin-only policy, written under her own token) instead of Twilio, and the widget polls that table through `yaad-inbound` while the page is open, using the visitor token as the only proof of who is asking. Polls are not model calls and are not throttled. The WhatsApp button stays beside it, because a reply into a closed tab reaches nobody and the desk note says so plainly. Second, the FAQ ("should also have the FAQ question if someone asks them"): the twelve answers from `docs/faq.html` are condensed into `faq.ts` and injected into the one intake prompt, so WhatsApp answers them too. That put published service prices into a model's mouth for the first time, which is not the pricing path §5 protects (a repair is still only ever quoted by a worker against a scope) but is still a number a model could garble; so `price-figures.ts` is the hold point: any pound, J$ or percent figure not on the published list is cut from the reply in code, on every channel, and a test proves `faq.ts` itself passes the guard so the two files cannot drift apart. Third, a backstop found by testing: "Can I talk to a real person please" once got the generic opener because the model call failed and `wants_human` was that call's only reading; a plain word match on the visitor's own text now adds the true the model missed, the same shape as the confirmation backstop. Testing the FAQ answers surfaced two more model habits, both handled in code rather than by hoping: the longer prompt made the model think longer, and 3500 tokens was no longer enough for the reasoning plus the answer (budget raised to 6000, and the think block is stripped before the JSON is looked for, so a brace inside the reasoning is never mistaken for the answer); and asked a plain question, the model sometimes answers in plain prose with no JSON at all, so prose of a sane length with no braces is now taken as the reply, with every classification field left empty, and still passes through the promise strip, the figure guard and the language screen like any other reply.

## Client photographs are signed out of the private bucket, and publishing one is a human decision (2 Sep 2026)

The board had never shown a real photograph. `job_photos.img` was the only column anything rendered and it has been null on every row a real person ever caused; the picture that made the board look like it worked was a JPEG baked into the app's own assets. Meanwhile WhatsApp intake had been doing the right thing since 27 August, putting the actual file in the private `intake` bucket and recording `storage_path` on the row, and nothing ever read it back.

The question put was a public `job-photos` bucket or signed URLs from the private one. **Signed URLs.** The photographs are already in the private bucket, so a public bucket means either a second home and a copy step or moving WhatsApp intake into the open, which `yaad-inbound` refuses in writing and correctly: a photograph of the inside of an often-empty house, next to a parish on the same card, is a catalogue. Signing is not a new mechanism here either, it is exactly what the evidence bucket has done in the portal and the desk since 30 August, so this is one more use of a proven path rather than a second way of doing storage. And a public URL cannot be withdrawn, where a signed one is dead in five minutes and a forwarded one is dead on arrival. The extra work was one signing step in pages that already loaded the rows.

**What that uncovered is the more important half.** app.yaadly.co.uk/jobs is a public page, and the policy in force said every photograph on an open job was world-readable. Nothing had leaked only because `img` was null. Rendering the pictures without touching that policy would have published every photograph a client sent into a private WhatsApp conversation, to anyone on the internet, in the same deploy, and it would have looked like the feature working. So `board_ok`, default false on every row including the ones already there: a photograph reaches the public board when a named human at the desk puts it there, which is §2 of CLAUDE.md applied to a consent decision rather than to money. The client and the booked worker see their own job's photographs either way, published or not, which is the second thing this fixed: the portal had been querying that table since it was built and getting nothing back the moment a job left the board. The client's board preview now marks which of their pictures the public actually carries, because "what exactly am I publishing?" was already that card's job and it had been answering it wrongly.

Two policies on `storage.objects` mint the links, one for the public board and one for the job's own party, both matched on the `job_photos` row and on the folder the object sits in. The folder check is not decoration: `storage_path` is free text and a client may insert a photo row against their own job, so without it a client could have named another job's file, set `board_ok`, and had the board sign somebody else's private photograph for them. The same insert policy now refuses a client-set `storage_path` or `board_ok` outright. Files are written by `yaad-inbound` on the service role and there is deliberately no insert, update or delete policy on that bucket for anyone else: a photograph a client sent as their account of the job is not something the other side of that job should be able to replace.

**Built the same evening, on her next message:** "there should be a link where people can upload photo, we used it in the worker job selection." The mechanism was already here twice, in `/apply` for a worker's documents and in the portal for evidence, so `20260902w` and `job-photo-actions.ts` are that same shape a third time rather than a third way of doing storage: the browser posts to a Server Action, the Server Action writes into the private bucket through the client's own session, and a Postgres policy decides whether that is allowed. The client's portal job page gained "Photos of the job", at every stage rather than only before a worker is booked, because a picture that would have helped a quote also helps the person who turns up.

The link is the one the client already has. `/portal/join?job=…&code=…` for somebody who has not set the portal up, which the wizard shows on the "job received" screen and `yaad-notify-client` messages out, then the job page itself. There is deliberately no tokenised upload URL that works without signing in: it would have been less work and it is a page that accepts photographs of somebody's house on the strength of a URL, which is a new public surface and a new decision, and the portal already solves the same problem with auth that exists. The wizard copy changed with it; it used to say the quickest way to send photos was WhatsApp, which was true and was also the whole gap.

Three details that are decisions rather than plumbing. The path prefix is `client/`, where the assistant writes `whatsapp/`, and the policy pins a client to their own prefix and their own job folder, so a client can never write into or shadow the folder `yaad-inbound` fills. The EXIF APP1 segment is stripped before anything is stored, exactly as evidence does it: a photograph of the inside of a house does not also need to carry its latitude, and this one may later go on a public board. And unlike evidence, these can be deleted by the person who sent them, right up until the desk publishes one; evidence is immutable because a fingerprint has to mean something, but a photograph of the job is the client's own account of it and somebody who has just sent a picture of their house to the wrong place must be able to take it back. After publishing, the client asks and a human takes it down, which is the same rule as everything else here.

**Still not built:** no photo upload inside the wizard itself, before the account exists. The wizard now points at the portal one screen later instead, which is the same thirty seconds and needs no anonymous upload surface.

## The client corrects their own description; a booked job's scope is the desk's to vary (3 Sep 2026)

Her ask, looking at her own test job reading "Fix the rood" in the board preview: a place to upload a picture and edit the description. The picture half already existed on the same page (20260902w, "Photos of the job"). The description had no way in at all, because `jobs` has never had an update policy for clients, and rightly: most of its columns belong to the desk or the state machine, and a blanket update policy hands a client `open`, `stage`, `worker_email` and the money columns in one line. So the edit is a function, `edit_job_descr_as_me()` (20260903a), the same shape as `nominate_materials_store()`: one column, one rule about who and when, refusal in words. The rule about when is the decision. Editing stops the moment a worker is booked, because from then on the description is the scope both sides confirmed a Kickoff Pack against, and changing the words under an agreement is a variation for a named human to record, not an edit; before that, quotes may be in, and the form says they were priced against the old wording rather than pretending otherwise. Deliberately not applied on the way in: the chat contact scrub. A description may legitimately carry an "Address:" or "Access contact:" line the desk and the booked worker need; `open_jobs` strips those before the board sees them, and the client's preview mirrors that chain, so the edit changes what is stored and the view still decides what is published. The editor sits directly above that preview on purpose, so an edit is read back immediately as a worker will read it.

## A photograph the client uploads is the client's to publish; a WhatsApp one stays the desk's (3 Sep 2026)

Her next instruction: "you should be able to upload photos also to the quote that will be live on the marketplace." The day before, publishing a photograph had been made a human decision at the desk (20260902v), and that was right for what existed then: every photograph had arrived inside a WhatsApp conversation, and a picture sent into a private chat is not consent to put it on a public page. A photograph the client uploads on their own job page with "show it on the marketplace" ticked is a different thing. The consenting person has consented, in the one place consent can be given, and a desk step there would be a human clicking what the client already said. So `20260903b` splits the rule by `source`: for rows the client sent, the client holds `board_ok` both ways and may withdraw the picture entirely, at upload or from a button on the thumbnail; for rows the assistant saved out of WhatsApp nothing changed, and Postgres refuses the client in words that say so. It is a function, `set_job_photo_board_as_me()`, not an update policy, because the only column a client should reach on that table is the one they are meant to reach, and an update policy would have handed them `storage_path`, which 20260902v records is how the board gets tricked into signing another job's file. The board and its storage policy did not change: the file-signing policy checks the folder is named after the job, not which prefix it is under, so a `client/` upload signs the same way a `whatsapp/` one does.

The checkbox is ticked by default, and that is the decision worth recording. The reason a client sends a photograph is so tradespeople can quote against it, and the marketplace is where they look, so a default of private would make the feature fail quietly for the people who most need it. The cost is real: these are often pictures of the inside of an empty house, shown next to a parish. It is mitigated three ways, none of them new. No address, name or phone number is on the board; the phone's location data is stripped before the file is stored; and the button on the thumbnail takes it off again in one click. If that trade turns out wrong in the pilot, flipping the default is one word in `JobPhotoUpload.tsx`.

## /apply says who it is for, counts three screens not nine, and survives losing the tab (3 Sep 2026)

Her brief: make the worker application understandable and credible for tradespeople, as an optimisation and not a rewrite. Mapping it first turned up more than copy.

**Two of the asks contradicted a decision already on the record, and both were resolved rather than routed around.** She asked for a progress indicator and the number of steps; the nine-item step rail was removed on 30 Aug for a stated reason still true today, that a list of nine things still to do is the first thing anybody sees and a reason to close the tab. What went in is not that rail. It is a counter scoped to the sitting you are standing in: three dots in Phase 1, four in Phase 2, and the count is read off the sitting's own length so it cannot drift from the screens that exist. It must never be made to span both sittings, because that is the deleted thing. She also asked not to invent approval times, and Phase 2's subtitle already promised "verified within 48 hours of your documents arriving", which promises the outcome rather than the reply. Softened to hearing back, which is what the confirmation screen has always said and what is actually controlled. Her call, taken.

**The worst defect was not a copy problem.** `remember()` ran on exactly three events: the application opening, a document landing, a Persona check recording. It never saved the step, the referees, the signature or the `continuing` flag, and `send()` cleared the whole store the instant Phase 1 was submitted. The only door into the second sitting is the button on the sent screen and that button needs the application id and the upload token, so losing the tab between the two sittings made the ID check unreachable: no reference, no token, nothing to attach a passport to, and no way forward except applying twice. Autosave now runs on a debounce as they type, the step and the sitting are saved, and the claim is deliberately kept past the first submit and cleared only when the second sitting finishes or the applicant presses "Start again". That trade is the decision: the upload token lives in `localStorage` for longer than it used to. It was always there during Phase 2 uploads, which need it, and the alternative is a tradesperson who cannot finish their own application.

**Three smaller things were wrong rather than merely thin.** The step names were debris from the nine-step design, numbered 1, 2, nothing, 3, 5, 7, nothing, with no 4 and no 6; `n` was also dead data, declared and never rendered, so it is now the name the counter prints, which keeps it honest by making it visible. The Worker Guidelines screen was labelled Phase 3, so the header above somebody reading and signing them read "Phase 3 · On the board · what your account can and cannot do once it is live"; signing is an act, not a state, and both remaining screens moved to Phase 2. `Phase` is now `1 | 2` and Phase 3's copy did not move, because it was already sitting beside the signature as `ON_THE_BOARD`, explaining what signing leads to. And the second sitting had no ending at all: pressing the final button set `sentRef`, which the sent screen ignores while `continuing` is true, so it appeared to do nothing immediately after somebody handed over their identity documents. It has its own confirmation now.

**What is new to the page and not to the system.** A "who this is for" panel and a what-you-need-now against what-you-need-later split, both stated before anybody types, and the sentence that was missing everywhere: applying does not get you work, it gets your application read by a person, and being read is not being accepted. Every line of it is something the code or the desk actually does. The sent screen gained a copyable reference, that same what-this-is-not paragraph, and a standing list drawn from `done`, which only ever ticks when a thing is true. Nothing here describes a check that does not exist; that rule is the file's own and it held.

**Three CSS collisions found while looking.** `.upl span{display:block}` and `.sigbox span{display:block}` both outranked `.src{display:inline-flex}` on specificity and stretched the little required-or-optional chips into full width bars. The mobile `.upbtn{width:100%}` rule was written for upload rows and was never scoped to them, so it also hit the "Add link" button and squashed the input beside it to about thirty pixels. And the sticky mobile Back/Continue bar has to yield to the Ask Yaadly chat pill, which is fixed bottom-right at z-index 2400 on every page in this app by founder instruction (2 Sep 2026): the space is reserved on the last button rather than on the bar, so the line explaining why Continue is grey still gets the full width.

**Deliberately not touched.** The data model, every server call, every validation rule, every compliance field, `LiveCapture` and its honesty about its own ceiling, the Persona path with its load-failure fallback and its escape hatch for a worker whose document the template refuses, and the ninety-day purge copy. Screen 1 remains the only gate in the flow.

**Found and left alone, because it is not a technical decision.** `AI_CONSENT_VERSION` and the AI-review consent question no longer exist in `JoinFlow.tsx`, only the comment describing them, and `submit` sends no `aiReviewConsent`. The server reads a missing field as `declined`, which is the correct safe reading, so `yaad-vetting-review` is currently never triggered for anybody and every application records a refusal. CLAUDE.md §6 still describes that control as live. Whether the question comes back, and in what words, is a legal decision and Monique's, so it needs its own change and its own consent version.

## The AI review question comes back, as v3, and it has to sit before the first send (3 Sep 2026)

Her instruction, immediately after being told the question had gone: add it back now. So this is her call taken, not a proposal.

**Why a new version and not a return to v2.** CLAUDE.md §6 says the consent wording and `AI_CONSENT_VERSION` move together, because a consent is only worth the sentence that earned it. v2's sentence named "your police record, proof of address, TRN, certificates and CV". The police record step no longer exists anywhere in this flow, so that sentence named a document nobody is asked for, and a photograph of the applicant is now collected, which is a face and had to be named as withheld. The wording therefore had to change, so the version had to change: `ai-review-v3`. It is strictly narrower than v2, so every existing v2 yes already covers everything done under v3, and the bump is not a re-consent exercise, it is so that somebody can later ask what a given yes actually said and get the right answer.

**Why it renders on two screens rather than one.** `submit` is what triggers `yaad-vetting-review`, and Phase 1 submits. A question living only on the Phase 2 identity screen would be answered after the only moment it could take effect, which is precisely how the page ended up sending "declined" for everybody. So it sits on Phase 1's work screen, with the CV, portfolio and certificates it covers, immediately before the first send; and again on the Phase 2 identity screen, beside the TRN and proof of address, because somebody who said no in two minutes flat should be able to change that when they are actually handing the paperwork over. One piece of state, one answer, sent on whichever submit happens.

**Every claim in the copy is checked against code, not against the old copy.** The withheld list is `IDENTITY_DOCS` in `yaad-vetting-review`, tested before the download so a withheld file is never fetched out of the bucket at all. What is actually sent is what survives that: proof of address, TRN, certificates, CV, portfolio. The recipient is named as NVIDIA, because "an AI model" tells a tradesperson nothing about which country their proof of address ends up in, and NVIDIA's hosted vision model is what that function calls today. Neither option is pre-selected, and the copy says out loud that no answer is read as no.

**Two contradictions the returning question exposed, both fixed here.** The sent screen said "Nothing about your documents is sent outside Yaadly", which was true only while the question was missing and every application recorded a refusal; with the question back it is false for anybody who agrees, and it sat two panels above a readback saying their paperwork went to NVIDIA. It is now conditional on what they chose. And the readback itself was reading `sentConsent`, which is set inside `send()` and defaults to declined, so a sent screen restored after a lost tab told somebody who had agreed that they had refused. What was actually sent is now persisted with the reference and restored with it. On a consent screen that is the worst class of copy bug available, and it was found by walking the reload path rather than the happy one.

## A photograph of the tradesperson, the reply time drops to 24 hours, and the introduction video that never once saved (3 Sep 2026)

Three founder instructions and one bug found underneath them.

**The photograph.** Her ask: a section where someone can upload a picture of themselves. It is a new `profile_photo` document type going into the same private bucket, hashed server-side, on the same ninety day clock as everything else. It is a face, so it went onto `IDENTITY_DOCS` in `yaad-vetting-review` in the same change that created it, deliberately: a face that reaches that function before it reaches that list is a face that has already been sent. The page shows the picture back in the profile preview from a local object URL, created only after the server confirms the upload, so a photograph that failed can never appear as though it landed; after a reload the preview honestly falls back to initials and says the picture is on file, because the bucket is not readable by a browser and there is nothing to fetch.

**What the copy may not say, which is the decision worth recording.** It is not called a profile photo and the page never claims it will appear on the public profile, because two things in the system say otherwise: `worker_profiles` has no photo column, so no code path publishes it, and `yaad-vetting-purge` deletes everything in that bucket ninety days after upload, so it could not serve as a lasting profile picture even if something did read it. It says what is true, that it reaches the person reading the application. Making it a real published profile photograph is a second change and it carries a decision that is not technical: a worker's face on a public page next to a parish, with a retention rule that has to outlive ninety days.

**Twenty four hours, not forty eight.** Her instruction. Changed in all four places on `/apply` that promised a reply and in the ntfy push that reminds her of it, because two different numbers across one flow is worse than either number. It is a tighter promise than the one that was there, made deliberately and by her.

**And the bug.** Adding a document type meant reading the live check constraint on `vetting_documents`, which turned out not to contain `intro_video` either. The thirty second introduction was added on 31 Aug and wired the whole way through: the browser records it, the upload function lists it, the file lands in the bucket, and the reviewer has withheld it from the model since the day it was written. The one place it was never added is the database. So every introduction ever recorded uploaded successfully and then failed on the insert, showed "Could not record that document", and left the file orphaned until the purge collected it. Nobody reported it because the row is optional and the failure looks like a bad connection. Both values go in together in `20260903g`. The lesson is in the migration comment: the migration history is not the schema, and this was found by asking the database.

## /apply stops promising a worker payment in 24 hours, because the document they sign says 3 working days (3 Sep 2026)

Flagged as a follow-up by the `/trades` work in PR #86 and confirmed by the founder the same day. The recruitment screen said "once the client approves the evidence you are paid within 24 hours", on the screen where a tradesperson decides whether joining is worth their time. The Worker Guidelines they are asked to sign two screens later, `web/lib/legal-copy.json`, say within **3 working days**, per stage, and say that Yaadly is not holding money at present so the client pays through the route agreed on the job.

**A recruitment page cannot promise a tradesperson something faster, or safer, than the document it then asks them to sign.** The guidelines already record why, in the correction note attached to that very section: v1.2 said the client's payment was "confirmed and held before you start", and v1.3 struck it because "that was not true, because holding is not switched on yet, and it was the sentence most likely to be relied on. Corrected here rather than quietly." The page had been left carrying the same class of claim.

So the panel now says what the signed document says: paid per stage, a stage signed off is a stage paid, within 3 working days of the client approving the evidence, by bank transfer, Lynk wallet or remittance pick-up. And the fact underneath it, which was on no screen of `/apply` at all: Yaadly is not holding money at present, the client pays through the agreed route, and that section of the guidelines changes and is re-signed when holding goes live. The guidelines say a worker is entitled to know that before they turn up; stating the fee and the speed while leaving it out is the omission v1.2 was corrected for.

**The 24 hours elsewhere on this page is a different clock** and is unrelated: it is how fast a person reads the application, tightened from 48 hours on the founder's instruction the same day. Two numbers, two meanings, and they must not be allowed to drift into each other. The payment figure follows `legal-copy.json`; if the signed guidelines change it, this panel changes with them.

**`service_catalogue` is a source of truth in name only if nothing keeps it synced with the decisions made about it.** `20260826_invoicing.sql`'s seed data and `specs/PRICING.md` were written the same day, 26 Aug 2026, and already disagreed: the spec renamed two products (Visual Check, Condition Report) and removed two others (Project Setup Pack, Document Pack Check, the second for a stated legal reason) that the seed insert never reflected. Nothing failed loudly because nothing checks the two documents against each other; the desk's own invoice dropdown and the public booking form both just read `active = true` and trusted it. `20260903h` fixed the names and deactivated the two removed rows, and the same commit strips their `<option>`s from `docs/services.html` and their keys from `yaad-book-service`'s `BOOKABLE` map, on the reasoning that "removed" in the spec means retired everywhere a client or the desk can reach them, not just off one card. It stopped short of touching the pence values: several rows (Visual Check, Condition Report, Oversight Retainer, all three Property Care tiers, Technical Sign-off) still hold numbers from before the 26 Aug pricing decision, which is a real-money change and, per the founder rule on this repository, hers to confirm before it goes in, not something to infer from a spec file and apply quietly.
## The site says Yaadly is principal, and three legal pages went up with their gaps marked (3 Sep 2026)

The public site described a venue where a client and a tradesperson transact and a platform sits in the middle holding money. That is not the business, and it is the reading the principal structure exists to avoid, so the copy moved: the client buys the job from Yaadly, Yaadly prices it and subcontracts a vetted worker, and the client does not contract with the worker. The specific sentences were "a vetted tradesperson quotes it" and "vetted workers quote it" (a client receiving a quote from a worker is a client contracting with that worker), "can see the money is real" and "the job is funded" on the worker panel (both describe a pot set aside for the worker; under the principal structure the money is Yaadly's and so is the commitment, and the reassurance to the worker is identical either way), and the page title "The Marketplace". Five uses of "escrow" in the trust-engine demo went with them: that word is banned outright by CLAUDE.md §8, it had been live on the page the whole time, and the 3 September brief driving this change did not catch it either. The "25% max deposit, ever" claim came off in all four places because the founder removed the deposit cap the same day and no replacement figure was set; the panel it anchored now shows an unquantified four-stage ladder, and the CSS that hard-coded a 25% bar width was deleted rather than left to be re-used. Alongside those, "0 Contractor ties" and the company-wide "Independence guarantee" card came off the services page as simply untrue on a managed job, where Yaadly does price, supply and manage the trade. The narrower per-job independence claims on that page were left standing deliberately: on a check job where the client has their own builder they are still true, and that is the founder's call to make, not a session's.

Three legal pages were published in the same change (`docs/privacy.html`, `docs/cancellation.html`, `docs/terms.html`), with nothing anywhere on the site linking to a legal document before this. The governing instruction was publish now, clear with the adviser after, on the reasoning that a page which exists and says plainly what is still being settled beats no page and beats invented legal text by further still. So every genuinely open question is a visible marked box rather than a plausible paragraph: retention periods, liability and its limits, the workmanship guarantee (deliberately not promised before it is insured, though the three UK principals checked all give twelve months in their own name, so that is the norm it will be measured against), governing law for United States and Canada clients, and the registered office address. The prescribed statutory wording is the sharpest case: the model cancellation instructions and model cancellation form are substantially set by Schedules 3 and 4 of the Consumer Contracts (Information, Cancellation and Additional Charges) Regulations 2013, legislation.gov.uk is blocked by this environment's egress policy, and an approximation written from memory is worse than a marked gap because a wrong version reads exactly like the real one. The 14 day right, how to exercise it, and the consequence of asking for work to start early are all stated in full regardless, and are honoured whether or not the form is on the page. Two disclosures on the privacy page are marked not to be softened: the text model is MiniMax, in China, until the Mistral move lands, and identity documents never reach any model at all. Supabase's region was confirmed as eu-west-3 (Paris) rather than published on assumption.

Two things this change deliberately did not do. The registered office address is still not in the footer, which leaves Regulation 25 of the Company, Limited Liability Partnership and Business (Names and Trading Disclosures) Regulations 2015 unmet: the current registered office is the founder's home address, and republishing it in a footer on every page is worse than the gap. That unblocks only when an AD01 filing moves it to a service address. And no Stripe payment links were added, because creating them needs the Stripe account and a secret key must never enter a chat session; the choice between Dashboard links (charged immediately, five minutes, loses the line "your card is not charged until you approve the work") and API-created links with `capture_method` set to `manual` (keeps the hold, and the hold is the differentiator) is recorded for the founder, unmade.

## The registered office waits for a service address, and £500 splits invoice from hold (3 Sep 2026)

Two founder instructions the same evening.

**The address.** Regulation 25 of the Company, Limited Liability Partnership and Business (Names and Trading Disclosures) Regulations 2015 requires a company's website to carry the part of the UK it is registered in, the registered number and the registered office address. The first two were in the footer; the third was left out because the registered office is the founder's home, and a footer on every page of a site fed by a 9,000 follower TikTok is a different kind of exposure from a Companies House record: indexed, cached, archived. She instructed that it be published anyway to close the regulation, it was, and within the same session she reversed and chose to take a registered office service address instead. Nothing reached `main`, so the address was never live. The site is therefore back to carrying no address, and the Regulation 25 gap on that third element stands as a known, accepted, temporary breach rather than a permanent publication of a home address. It closes when the service address is taken (an "appropriate address" under the 4 March 2024 rules, so not a PO Box, about £30 to £50 a year) and an AD01 is filed within 14 days. `RUNBOOK.md` carries the three places the address goes and the limit that the historic register entry cannot be undone either way. The reversal is recorded rather than tidied away because the reasoning, that the cheaper compliance route costs something no policy page can give back, is the part worth keeping.

**The £500 threshold.** The morning's resolution was that payment terms are agreed in writing per job with no site-wide rule, which settled the terms but left the instrument unsettled, and the 3 September brief had flagged the consequence: a job paid by card hold produces no invoice, and the invoice was the artefact evidencing the principal structure, each one saying the client bought the job from Yaadly. The threshold resolves it. Above £500 the job is invoiced, so every larger job has that record. At or under £500 the card is authorised at booking and captured on the client's approval. The two rules coexist rather than contradict: price picks the instrument, the written terms still govern stages and amounts. Two constraints were carried into the copy. First, no Stripe links exist yet, so the hold half is not live and all three pages say every job is invoiced for now; that sentence comes out when the links do, not before. Second, "your card is not charged until you approve the work" is true only of a hold, so it is scoped to the at-or-under-£500 clause everywhere it appears and must never sit beside an invoice job. Under £500 there is still no invoice, so the written confirmation issued at booking has to carry the principal statement, which it must go out to do anyway under the distance selling durable-medium requirement.

## Principal, plumbed: one all-in client invoice and a worker payable (3 Sep 2026)

Founder's instruction, "build to principal now", after the structure question was settled earlier the same day. The trigger was her asking how she takes her 12% from the worker, and the honest answer being that she could not, because nothing had been built to.

**What was live and wrong.** Two documents per job. `raise_job_agency_fee_invoice()` billed the client 15% of labour. `raise_job_worker_pay_invoice()` billed the client the worker's **full** labour plus materials, with the note "no fee added, pay them directly". Meanwhile the worker's own screens showed him his labour **less 12%** under the words "You receive". So on one job the client was told to pay him 100% and he was told he would get 88%, and no invoice type, `payable_to` value or function anywhere collected the difference. The 12% was decorative. No real payment data exists in the repo, so nobody was actually shortchanged; it was found before a worker found it.

**Why principal dissolves it instead of plumbing it.** There is no fee to collect from a subcontractor. Yaadly engages him at his quoted price less 12% and pays him that, so the 12% is never collected because it is never paid out. Nothing of his passes through Yaadly, which is the entire point: taking money from one party to pass to another can be a regulated payment service under the Payment Services Regulations 2017, while buying work and reselling it is ordinary trading. The arithmetic is unchanged for everyone. Client pays labour plus 15% plus materials at cost; worker is paid labour less 12% plus materials at cost; Yaadly's margin is 27% of labour with materials passed through untouched.

`raise_job_client_invoice()` and `raise_job_worker_payable()` replace the pair, and the old two are dropped outright rather than left unused, following the precedent 20260901y set with `raise_job_stage_invoice`: a working function that computes the wrong money is worse to leave lying around than no function at all.

**One security consequence that was easy to miss.** Under the old shape the worker-pay invoice carried the *client's* email, correctly, because the client was paying it. Under this shape the client must never read that document: it is Yaadly's cost base, and `invoices_client_read` matches on `lower(client_email)` against the caller's JWT email, so leaving the client there would have handed them Yaadly's margin. `client_email` is NOT NULL, so a worker payable now carries a sentinel at a `.invalid` address, a TLD RFC 2606 reserves precisely so it can never be a real mailbox or match a real session. The worker still reads his own through `worker_email` and `invoices_worker_read`, untouched.

**On telling the worker there is a cut,** which the founder asked for explicitly. The words changed, the numbers did not. "Your fee, 12%" and "You receive" described money of his being reduced, which is the agent shape. It now reads "Yaadly's margin, 12% on labour" and "Yaadly pays you", with the 12% still shown in full on the quote screen before he accepts, because that is the only moment he is deciding anything. His portal now says Yaadly engages him at his quoted price less 12%, agreed before he accepts and never changed afterwards. He can still see that Yaadly makes 15% on the client side and 12% on his, which is unusually open and is a deliberate trust choice, not an oversight.

**What this does not do.** It changes what the documents say, not how money moves: both are still admin-raised and admin-marked-paid off platform. `docs/payments.html` still promises no client payment is taken until legal sign-off and insurance are in hand, and that promise is untouched, because the moment the client pays Yaadly for the whole job is the moment Yaadly carries the liability. `raise_job_stage_worker_pay_invoice(job, stage)` still apportions the old shape across payment stages and needs the same treatment; it reads the Kickoff Pack and the quote pack draft to do it, so it deserves its own review rather than a rewrite in passing. Recorded in RUNBOOK.md.

## Payment moves to principal, live, on the founder's word (3 Sep 2026)

Her instruction, verbatim in substance: legal sign-off and insurance are in hand, move forward with payment, she will gather the legal structure, and this is not to be a hold. The published promise that no client payment would be taken until both were in hand has therefore been met and comes off the site, along with every "you pay your tradesperson directly" statement across the marketing pages, the FAQ's structured data, the WhatsApp assistant's facts and the client notification texts.

**The replacement claim is deliberately the founder's own, and it is smaller than the one it replaces.** "Standing behind the work" was the old sentence. She said what she actually offers is that Yaadly reviews the works and ensures issues are handled, on small jobs where the risk is contained, so that is what the site now says. No workmanship guarantee period is stated anywhere, because none has been set and promising a length before it is insured for that length is how a guarantee becomes a liability. The placeholder on `docs/terms.html` stays.

The counter-argument was put to her once and she is aware of it: small jobs cap the price, not the exposure. As principal the client's remedy is against Yaadly, so on a £400 plumbing job the worst case is the flooded house, not the £400. Her own 3 September brief made the same point in its opening correction. That is a founder risk judgment, taken knowingly, and it is hers.

Two things did not switch on with it. Card payment is still not live because no Stripe links exist, so every job is invoiced and paid by bank transfer and all three pricing surfaces still say so. And `CLAUDE.md` section 9 still parks payment integration until the legal review lands, which is now stale; that file is Monique's and a session must not rewrite it to suit a task, so it is flagged for her rather than edited.

One regression from earlier the same day was caught and fixed in the same change. The catalogue price alignment updated `docs/services.html` and `docs/faq.html` but not `price-figures.ts`, the exact-figure allowlist that guards what the assistant may say. Its own comment says to update it in the same commit as `faq.ts`, and that was missed, so the assistant would have had every sentence carrying £249 or £45 cut before sending, leaving a client asking about prices with no price at all. The list now carries all twelve published figures, and the runbook records that `service_catalogue`, the services page, `faq.ts` and this list are four copies of one fact.

## Two lanes, named, and the payment sentence rewritten across docs/ and web/ (4 Sep 2026)

A copy audit of all eleven public marketing pages found four different money stories on one site. The largest single finding read, at first, like a flat contradiction: `docs/business.html` said in bold, twice, "**Yaadly holds none of your money.** You pay your contractor directly", while `docs/index.html`, `docs/marketplace.html`, `docs/faq.html` and `docs/payments.html` all said the client pays Yaadly and Yaadly pays the tradesperson.

**Monique's answer was that both are true, and that the business page describes a real second offer**: the client keeps their own contractor and Yaadly sells only the eyes. So the site did not have one money story to fix. It had **two true money stories and no vocabulary for telling them apart**, and pages had been reaching for whichever one fitted the paragraph. That is the decision this change is built on, and it is now written down once, in `docs/COPY-GUIDELINES.md`, which is the source of truth for customer facing wording across `docs/` and `web/app/`.

The two lanes: a **managed job**, where Yaadly is principal, the client buys the job from Yaadly at one agreed price, and Yaadly engages and pays the tradesperson as its subcontractor; and **oversight only**, where the contractor stays the client's, the client pays them directly, Yaadly holds none of the money for the building work, and Yaadly invoices only its own fee. Six of the seven priced services on `docs/services.html` are oversight only. Full Project Management is the one managed job on that page, which is why the page read as two products stapled together.

**The nuance that must not be dropped.** On the oversight lane Yaadly still invoices its own fee, and on Property Care the attending person is Yaadly's. So the sentence is "Yaadly holds none of the money for the building work", never the bare "Yaadly holds none of your money", because the client is holding an invoice from Yaadly while they read it.

**The payment sentence itself was wrong, and not only in tone.** Six surfaces said some form of "nobody is paid until you approve", including `web/app/jobs/page.tsx`, whose own comment recorded it as a deliberate 3 September correction of an earlier holding claim. It fixed that one and introduced a worse one. Under the principal contractor model the client does not contract with the tradesperson, so they have no power to release that person's pay, and `docs/terms.html` already said so. The client **accepts the work**; Yaadly then pays its own subcontractor. Two acts, collapsed into one everywhere except the terms page. Every surface now says both, and the FAQ's `FAQPage` JSON-LD was updated in step with its visible copy, since it is published twice.

**Nothing about the human gate changed.** `approve_stage()` in Postgres is untouched, `ApproveButton` still posts to it, and a named person still confirms every payment. What changed is the label: "Yes, approve and release payment" became "Yes, accept this stage", which is what the button has always actually done. `CLAUDE.md` sections 2 and 3 were the reason to be careful here, and the request that would have violated them (make approval fewer clicks) was not made and was not invented.

**One claim was removed because the system does not back it.** `web/app/workers/page.tsx` and `web/components/WorkerDirectory.tsx` told every visitor that each listed worker had passed "government photo ID on a video call, and references called", and that "nobody is listed before that is done". `trg_profile_publish_checks` enforces exactly three things before a profile can be published: a Persona identity check reading approved or completed, a TRN reading approved, and an email. Not a video call, and not references. The copy now claims the two checks the trigger actually enforces, and states the three-referees rule as the operational policy it is (before any job over £500, any occupied home, any key-holding job), which is how `docs/faq.html` had it right all along. `docs/marketplace.html` carried the same overclaim and now matches.

**Prices were contradicting each other across three pages**, and two of them presented a founding rate identical to the standard rate, which the FAQ names by statute as a misleading action under the Digital Markets, Competition and Consumers Act 2024. The figures were aligned to the ones already published on the homepage and in the FAQ rather than invented: Deposit Protection Check £149 against a standard £249, Visual Check £95 against £125. That is a price decision and it is flagged to Monique as one; it was taken in the conservative direction, using numbers already promised to customers in public, because leaving a fake discount up was the worse of the two risks.

Also in this change, and smaller: `services.html#prices` was a dead anchor linked from two pages; `business.html` booked at `cal.com/yaadly` where the rest of the site used `cal.com/yaadly/15min`; the site made four different promises about reply speed and now makes one; `docs/prices.html` carried no Yaadly price at all despite being labelled "What work costs", and now carries the fee table, which also gives the 15 and 12 per cent split a findable home instead of one dense paragraph; `faq.html` had no headings at all and its accordions now carry `role="heading" aria-level="2"`; and `docs/payments.html`, `docs/services.html` and `docs/terms.html` each now say plainly that Yaadly does not operate an escrow service, which no page had ever said.

**Two things left for Monique out of this change, both flagged rather than done.**

First, `supabase/functions/_shared/guardrails.ts` bans the token "escrow" and its guidance says to use **"held safely with a licensed payment provider"** instead. That replacement is now the wrong advice: under the principal contractor model Yaadly does not hold money for anybody, so the phrase asserts something untrue, and `docs/COPY-GUIDELINES.md` bans it alongside "escrow". The banned-terms list was not touched, because it is the mechanism `CLAUDE.md` sections 2 and 3 exist to protect, it lives in two runtimes that must move together, and the "merchant of record, never escrow" wording is a legal positioning decision from the ideas ledger rather than a technical one. It needs her call, and the guidance string is the only part that looks wrong; banning the word itself is still right.

Second, the price alignment (Deposit Protection Check to £149 against a standard £249, Visual Check to £95 against £125) is a money decision under `CLAUDE.md` section 10. It was taken in the conservative direction, adopting figures already published to customers on the homepage and in the FAQ rather than inventing any, because leaving a founding rate identical to its standard rate was the larger risk of the two. If either standard figure is wrong, that is hers to correct, and `RUNBOOK.md` now lists all seven places a price has to change together.

**A guardrail caught a mistake in this change and was obeyed.** The first version of the assistant's facts in `supabase/functions/yaad-inbound/faq.ts` said "Yaadly does not operate an escrow service", copying the sentence added to the marketing pages. `price-figures_test.ts` failed, because the screen bans the token regardless of the sentence around it, and that is correct: putting the word in front of a client over WhatsApp is what ledger guardrail 1 forbids, denial or not. The copy was changed, not the assertion. The marketing pages keep the explicit sentence, because `guardrails.scan` does not read them and the founder asked for it by name; outbound assistant text says the same thing without the word. This asymmetry is deliberate and is now noted in the copy guidelines.

## A client can ask for one worker by name, and gets first refusal, not a booking (5 Sep 2026)

The "Book <name> for a job" button on a worker profile has existed since the
marketplace was built and did almost nothing. It carried `?worker=<slug>` into
the job wizard, printed the name on the confirmation screen, and added one
line of free text to the enquiry email. Nothing reached the job row. The job
went onto the open board like any other, every worker in that trade could
quote it, the worker who had been asked for was never told, and the only trace
of the client's choice was a sentence somebody had to read and act on by hand.
The screen said "we will take this to them first" and no part of the system
knew that promise had been made.

Three shapes were put to the founder: hold the job for that worker until they
answer, hold it for a fixed window, or name the request but leave the job on
the open board from the start. She chose the middle one. The job is held off
the board for 48 hours, only the requested worker can quote it, and the hold
ends the moment any of three things happens: they quote, they decline, or the
clock runs out. Holding it until they answer would have left a client waiting
indefinitely behind somebody who was simply busy; leaving it open from the
start would have made the button a label rather than a promise.

Three things worth writing down about the shape. There is no "accept" state:
accepting without quoting changes nothing for the client, and a state that
changes nothing is one more thing to teach, so the worker's yes IS their
quote. There is no stored "expired" state either, only a computed one, which
means no scheduled job has to run for a hold to lapse and no cron failure can
quietly hold a job shut forever. And none of this books anybody: `worker_email`
on a job is still written in one place, `_do_choose_worker`, when the CLIENT
accepts a quote. This holds a door open for one person for two days.

The client hears when the worker declines and does not hear when the window
simply lapses. That asymmetry is deliberate. A decline is something the worker
said; a lapse is silence, and turning silence into "your worker did not answer"
would be Yaadly reporting on somebody's reliability off a two day clock, which
is close to a reputation judgement and is not a trigger's to make. The wizard's
confirmation copy says exactly this, so the promise and the code agree.

`20260905a_a_client_asks_for_one_worker_and_that_worker_hears_about_it.sql`.

## A worker's own face, voice and work go on their profile, on a second consent, as a copy (5 Sep 2026)

/apply has collected a photograph of the tradesperson, a thirty second
introduction video and a portfolio file since 3 September. All three land in
the private vetting bucket, on the ninety day purge clock, and no client has
ever seen one. The public profile's Portfolio section is a different thing: it
is built from completed Yaadly jobs, and at launch every worker's is empty. So
a client choosing who to let onto their mother's roof was looking at a trade,
a parish and a list of checks, and nothing of the person.

Asked whether to build a new upload or publish what /apply already collects,
the founder chose the second. Two things stood in the way, and neither is
solved by pointing the page at the private bucket.

The first is consent. The photograph row on /apply ends with the sentence
"This page does not publish it anywhere", and that sentence was true and was
read by everybody who has applied so far. Publishing on the strength of it
would be answering a question nobody was asked. So there is a second consent,
with its own version (`SHOWCASE_CONSENT_VERSION`, `showcase-v1`), neither
option pre-selected, unanswered read as no, and the test lives in the database
view rather than at any call site, so no future page can forget it. Withdrawing
consent empties a profile on the next page load, before any file is deleted.

The second is the purge. `yaad-vetting-purge` destroys the file ninety days
after it arrives. A profile video served out of that bucket would have worked
for three months and then quietly gone missing. So the consented file is
COPIED into a separate public bucket and the vetting original keeps its clock
and is destroyed on time, exactly as the applicant was told. Nothing about the
private bucket, its policies or its purge changed, which was the point: the ID
rules in CLAUDE.md section 6 are the last thing that should move to make a
profile page look better.

Publishing is a button at the desk, never automatic, and `published_by`
records the person who pressed it. The desk signs the private originals for an
hour so that person can look at each file before deciding, because publishing
somebody's face on the open internet is a decision and a decision made without
looking is not one.

The self-supplied showcase renders in its own sections, above the evidence
portfolio and labelled "Work they showed us", saying plainly that it is not
from jobs booked through Yaadly and does not carry the evidence trail. That
labelling is the whole design. The section below it earns its trust by being
pulled from the evidence record, and borrowing that sentence for material a
worker handed in would spend the credibility of the checked thing on the
unchecked one.

`20260905b_a_worker_gets_a_face_a_voice_and_work_to_show.sql`.

## The vision provider moved into `_shared`, 5 September 2026

`textmodel.ts` exists because the text provider was a property of eight files
rather than a decision. The vision provider was still in exactly the state that
file was written to fix: `https://integrate.api.nvidia.com/v1/chat/completions`
typed out in `yaad-notify-client`, `yaad-sketch` and `yaad-vetting-review`. So
the question "which country do site photographs, a walkthrough of somebody's
house and a worker's proof of address travel to" was answerable only by
grepping, and changing the answer meant editing three files and deploying three
functions. The CI rule that was meant to prevent precisely this watched only
the text endpoints, so it stood green over the more sensitive half of the
estate, which is worse than no rule because it reads as covered.

`_shared/visionmodel.ts` now resolves the provider, the key and the model for
all three, in the same shape as `textmodel.ts`: an explicit
`VISION_MODEL_KEY`/`VISION_MODEL_API` override first, then NVIDIA, then null.
There is deliberately no third branch and no fallback, for the reason
`textmodel.ts` gives: a provider that is no longer the choice is a silent route
to a country nobody chose, waiting on one missing secret. The CI rule now
covers the vision endpoint too, which it could not do while three functions
still held the string.

Two things changed beyond the move. Each vision job got its own model secret,
because all three read `NVIDIA_VISION_MODEL` while defaulting to different
models, so with the secret unset they diverged by accident and with it set they
moved together whether or not that was intended. Reading a client's evidence
photographs, describing rooms, and reading dates off a utility bill have
different tolerances for being wrong, and the evidence review, which is the
vision call closest to a stage approval, was the one running the smallest
default. The per-job defaults reproduce exactly what each call site resolved to
before, so the move altered no behaviour on the day it landed; deciding what
the evidence review should actually run on is a founder call, recorded in
RUNBOOK.

Second, every vision span now carries `yaadly.model.region`. The text spans
already did. The one class of call that sends actual photographs was the class
that could not be traced to a country, which is the wrong way round.

None of this touches what the vision agents are allowed to do. The evidence
review still describes what is visible and hands it to a person; it does not
grade evidence as sufficient and it does not gate a stage. `yaad-vetting-review`
keeps its consent gate inside `review()` and keeps `IDENTITY_DOCS` withheld
before download. A better model is a more accurate description in front of the
named human, never a decision made further from them.

## The staging prefix is swept on a clock, and the abandoned session goes with it (5 Sep 2026)

`yaad-inbound` stages an inbound WhatsApp photo at `evidence/_pending/<uuid>`
before it knows which job it belongs to, then moves it to `<job_id>/<uuid>`
once the worker has answered the job code and the context question. A move is
a rename, so anything left in that prefix is
evidence nobody ever answered for. Nothing removed it.

The prefix was checked before any of this was written, and it held zero
objects. That is the honest number, and on its own it argues for doing nothing.
It was still built and still scheduled, for two reasons. The leak is
prospective rather than historical: abandonment is a December behaviour, not an
August one, and the prefix is empty now precisely because almost nobody has
used the lane yet. And a purge that exists but is never called is the exact
failure `20260827f` was written to record, where every vetting document carried
a deletion date that nothing acted on for a month. Writing the function without
the schedule would have repeated it deliberately.

So it follows `yaad-vetting-purge` rather than inventing a second pattern: an
Edge Function with two doors, a signed-in admin or a secret checked against a
SHA-256 hash in `app_settings`, called nightly by pg_cron through pg_net,
because the job lives inside the database and cannot read the function's
environment. Its own secret rather than the purge's, so rotating one does not
silently break the other.

Three things make a deletion safe, and the interesting one is not the age.
72 hours clears the 48 the intake code already treats as stale, so it can never
race a live conversation. No `public.evidence` row may claim the path, which
structurally can never happen because finalising renames the object out of the
prefix, and is there as a belt against a future writer that files a staged path
directly. The load-bearing check is the third: no intake session touched in the
last 72 hours may still name the path, whatever the object's own age says. That
makes it safe by construction rather than safe by the margin between two
numbers.

It deletes the abandoned session row too, which looks like scope creep and is
not. `yaad-inbound` intends to drop a stale evidence session at 48 hours, but
that branch sits after the evidence branch and returns before reaching it, so
for an evidence session it never runs and nothing else deletes the row. Had the
sweep taken the files and left the row, a worker returning on day five would be
asked what their photo shows, answer, and be told "Confirmed, but nothing saved
properly", a failure the sweep would have introduced. Taking both means that
worker is read fresh, which is what the 48 hour rule was written to do. Only
the evidence lane; the other lanes hold no staged files.

Nothing in it rules on anything. It deletes working state that the code already
meant to discard, and touches no job, no filed evidence, no money and no Yaad
Score.

`supabase/functions/yaad-evidence-sweep/`,
`20260906013700_the_staging_prefix_gets_a_sweep.sql`.
## One evidence email per stage, and why fired_at could not answer it (5 Sep 2026)

Founder's instruction, on seeing what tagging photographs would do: it should
go to the email once, not twice.

The existing debounce covered a burst. Every evidence insert reset a 90 second
timer, so five photographs sent back to back produced one email. What it never
covered was two batches an hour apart: the open-timer index is partial, so once
a timer fires the next photograph opens a fresh one, and the stage is still
status 'evidence' because nobody has approved it, so it fires again.

That was occasional until today. Asking a worker which phase a photograph is
(`20260906000700`) makes two batches per stage, a before and later an after,
the ordinary shape of a job, so building that and leaving this would have been
shipping the annoyance on purpose.

The rule is now one evidence email per job and stage. Anything filed afterwards
lands silently and the client sees it when they open the stage they have already
been told to look at. The cost is real and is the right way round: a client who
reads the email when the before lands is not pinged when the after arrives, and
two emails carrying the same sentence about the same stage is how people learn
to ignore both. The approval that releases money is something they come back to
the portal for.

The interesting part is why `fired_at` could not be used to answer "has this
stage already been emailed". `yaad-evidence-landed-check` stamps it on both
paths: one when it sends, and one when it clears a stale timer silently because
the stage was approved, disputed or moved past inside the 90 seconds. Reading it
as a delivered email would have treated a silently cleared timer as a
notification and suppressed the real one. So `notified_at` was added and records
only sends.

`mark_evidence_landed_fired` was dropped and recreated with a defaulted second
argument rather than overloaded, because a two-argument version alongside the
one-argument version makes the call PostgREST already makes ambiguous, and that
failure would land exactly when a notification was due. The default is false so
the currently deployed edge function keeps behaving as it does today until it is
redeployed: it records no sends and suppresses nothing. A change that decides
whether a client hears from us at all should fail towards the old behaviour, not
towards silence.

`20260906015600_the_evidence_email_goes_once_per_stage.sql`,
`supabase/functions/yaad-evidence-landed-check/index.ts`.

## Two sessions built the same thing, and what survived (5 Sep 2026)

Worth recording because the failure is procedural, not technical, and it will
happen again.

Two Claude sessions were asked for evidence sectioning on the same day and both
built it. One shipped `evidence.phase` with before, during, after, issue, plus
materials read off `kind`, and merged as PR #125. The other built
`evidence.purpose` with before, after, issue, new, plus an explicit before-to-
after pairing. Neither could see the other's branch. CLAUDE.md §12 warns about
exactly this and the second session only used it for naming migration files,
not for checking whether the work already existed.

Founder's call on being shown both: `phase` survives, because it was merged
first, is already in the stage approval snapshot, and unpicking it would be
worse than unpicking the other. The duplicate column was dropped before it was
ever applied. What was rebuilt on top of `phase` is the part `phase` did not
have.

The lesson for a future session is one line: fetch and read `origin/main`
before building, not just before naming a file.

## A new find is not a problem, and an after answers a named before (5 Sep 2026)

Two things `20260906000700` did not have.

**`new` as a fifth section.** `issue` was carrying two different things and the
difference between them is money. A problem with work already in scope and
already priced is included; putting it right changes nothing the client pays.
Something discovered that was never in the job has been quoted by nobody and
agreed by nobody, and it may change the price and the timeline. The tell that
they had been conflated was in the copy: the client-facing note under `issue`
read "not part of what was originally quoted", which was only ever true of half
of what was filed there. That note now belongs to `new`, and `issue` keeps its
own promise about writing so nothing a client reads lost it.

The order test in `web/tests/evidence-sections.test.mjs` was updated to expect
five. That is a test encoding a product decision, and the decision changed. It
is not an assertion weakened to make a broken change pass, and the money promise
it guards now has to hold on two notes instead of one.

**The pairing.** An after names the before it answers, by `item_code`. That
code already existed: `20260831zzzz2` added P1, P2, P3 so one WhatsApp reply
could name one photograph instead of a whole stage, and this is the second
thing it turned out to be for. The rules are enforced in a trigger rather than
a CHECK, because every one of them is about another row: an after only,
pointing at a before, on the same job, never at itself. `on delete set null`
rather than cascade, because if a before ever goes, the after is still real
evidence of real work.

The comparison is shown inside the after's own card, as a thumbnail of the
before, rather than by drawing the before as a second full card. A record that
lists the same photograph twice is a worse record even when it looks better.

**N moved from "none of those" to "new", and skipping became S.** A letter that
already meant something else is a real cost, and the reason it moved anyway is
that "N for new" is the mnemonic a worker will reach for, while skipping is the
rare answer. A worker who replies N meaning to skip gets a new find, and the
desk corrects it. Nothing about it blocks a filing either way.

**The stage is named before anything is sent.** Founder's point, and it is
about timing rather than information: a worker does not know which stage a job
is on, the stages move while they are working, and finding out from the
confirmation afterwards is too late to say otherwise. The proportion of the
money a stage carries is stripped out of that sentence, because what a stage is
worth is not a thing to put in front of somebody mid-job.

**A correction cannot be anonymous.** `retag_evidence` refuses when it cannot
tell who is signed in, and stamps `phase_set_by` and `phase_set_at`. A worker's
answer is their claim about their own photograph, not a ruling, so the desk
correcting a wrong one is ordinary and expected. Doing it silently would not be:
this is evidence, and overwriting what somebody said with no trace of who
changed it makes the record less trustworthy than leaving the mistake in. A
correction never touches the image, the description or the fingerprint.

`20260906020400_a_discovery_is_not_an_issue_and_an_after_answers_a_before.sql`,
`web/lib/portal/evidence-sections.ts`, `supabase/functions/yaad-inbound/index.ts`,
`concierge/concierge.html`.

## The measurement rule was tested, and it was narrower than its sentence (5 Sep 2026)

**What was found.** The sketch pack rule, "a sketch never states a dimension", was enforced three times over and all three layers agreed with each other on every one of forty-one test sentences. That was the problem. The pattern they agreed on missed twelve of twenty-nine real measurements, and the miss that matters is one character wide: "a 2-metre crack" walked through while "a 2 metre crack" was caught. A fluent model writes the hyphenated form. Number words ("twelve feet by ten feet", "half a metre") and a handful of units (acres, yds, square yards, ft2, km) also went through, and the scrubber left "6'" sitting in a sentence after it had removed the inches next to it. Separately, `sketch_offending_text()` scanned room names, notes, captions and the drawing, and not `property_label`, which is the title printed at the top of the client's document.

**Why nobody noticed.** The rule existed as three hand-typed copies: `yaad-sketch`, `yaad-report` and the migration. The report copy's own comment said it was "deliberately the same regex, because two copies of that rule would drift", and then typed it out again. The Postgres tests were run by hand and never in CI, and the TypeScript scrubber had no test at all. Three consistent copies of a narrow rule look exactly like a wide rule from the outside.

**What changed.** One TypeScript copy, `_shared/measurements.ts`, imported by both functions. The Postgres copy is now the same string inside `$re$` dollar quotes, and `measurements_test.ts` reads the newest migration defining `has_measurement()` on every push and fails if the text differs by one character. That test cannot reach the live database, so the runbook carries a one-line hash comparison for the day it matters. The pattern itself gained the hyphen, the number words, and the missing units. The scrubber was rewritten to put the sentence's own boundary characters back rather than eat the next token, so "200 sq ft" no longer becomes "[size removed] ft". The scan reads `property_label`. Nothing was loosened: every sentence refused before is refused now, and the forty-nine-sentence case list asserts it in both runtimes.

**Two things left out, on purpose.** Typographic dashes are not in the separator class. Nothing in this system produces one, the house writing rule forbids them, and keeping the pattern to plain characters is what lets the migration and the module carry byte-identical text. And "meter" after a number word is not a unit, because "two meters on the outside wall" is the electricity meter and blocking an approval over it would be a false positive at the one gate that is meant to be reliable. Digits still catch "3 meters". Beyond both: no pattern catches "about the length of the veranda", and the rule was never the last line. The named human reading the pack is.

**Applied live the same day**, function by function through the SQL console, and verified against the repo by hash rather than by eye, because a version with literal dashes went in first by hand and had to be replaced. That is the failure the hash line in the runbook exists for.

`supabase/functions/_shared/measurements.ts`, `measurements_test.ts`,
`supabase/migrations/20260906030000_the_measurement_rule_learns_hyphens_and_words.sql`,
`supabase/functions/yaad-sketch/index.ts`, `supabase/functions/yaad-report/index.ts`,
`supabase/tests/sketch_guards.sql`.

## The MiniMax fallback comes back, narrower, and it says so out loud (5 Sep 2026)

**The instruction, and the pushback.** Monique asked for MiniMax as the fallback when the Mistral key does not load. The concern was put to her plainly: a silent failover means that on any evening Mistral is unconfigured, real client and worker text goes to China and nothing says so, which is the exact outcome the 4 September move existed to end. She reaffirmed it. That is a data protection decision and it is hers, so it was built, and this paragraph exists so the next session does not "fix" it back out or, worse, widen it.

**What came back is not what was removed.** The 4 September branch was a third provider reached whenever the Mistral key was missing, with nothing to distinguish "we chose this" from "a secret fell off". The reinstated branch is conditioned three ways. It is reached only when `MISTRAL_API_KEY` is absent, never when Mistral is present and failing: a 429 or a wrong model id still retries once and fails loudly, because a fallback that catches provider errors would route every Mistral hiccup to China and that was not what was asked for.
